RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Annual Meeting. The first meeting of the newly-elected Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. There were present :—Mestrs. C. Roylance, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, P. Mounfield, J. Whitlow, A. Broome, T. 'Baxter. H. N. Davies, C. E. Linaker, T. Lewis, J. Smith, T. Wright, H. Mawdsley, J. T. Coiner, S. Woodward, W. Harrison, G. Pilling, S. Horton, J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, C. Horton, R. A. Chrimes, 3.1 Pryer, J. Spruce, W. Fletcher, J. Hallows, R. Billington, C. E. Linaker, jun., J. Mountield, W. Turner, 3. B. Chadwick, A. G. Guest, T. Ant,ivis, J. Whitley, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (deputy clerk), Dr. Adams (medical officer of health), J. Swinton and W. Diggle (surveyors), and J. Farrington (inspector of nuisances). CHAIRMAN AND VICE-CHAIRMAN RE-ELECTED. Mr. C. E. Linaker, after expressing gratification at the large attendance said that. as far as his experience went, the Council he'd been singularly fortunate in hav- ing good chairmen. The late Mr. Knowles and Mr. Mclntyre were exemplary chairmen, and he thought the gentleman whose name he should propose had justified his re-election. In Mr. Woodward they had had a very good chairman during the past year. (Hear, hear.) He had presided over them with good temper, discretion, and very good judgment. Something more was required of a man who aspired to be chairman of a Council such as that, than simply to sit lathe chair and occasionally make remarks. He wanted to have a good grasp of the business, together with patience and tact to control the members,especially when the discussions became heated. He proposed that Mr. Woodward be elected chairman for the ensuing twelve mohths. Mr. Wright seconded. Mr. Woodward, he said, had been a splendid chairman, and he (Mr. Wright) was tare he would discharge the duties equally well in the future. The motion was carried with acclamnaation. In returning thanks, Mr. Woodward said it would have heen impossible for him to discharge his duties successfully if he had not been supported by the mem- bers of the Council generally. He was quite sure the kindly consideration he bad received from the old Council would be accorded to him by the present Council. There was a saddening element in the meeting, in-as- much as they were parting with a number of old members, some of whom had left them under rather pathetic circumstances. They all sympathised with Mr. Mclntyre, a former chairman of the Council and of the Board of Guardians,in his retirement. They extended a cordial welcome to the new members. There was plenty of work for them to do, and the pay was almost as good as the work. (Laughter.) He would like to have the honour and privilege of nominating Mr. George Pilling as vice-chairman for the year. Mr. Baxter seconded and the motion was heartily greed to. Mr. Pilling briefly returned thanks for the honour conferred upon him. APPOIN"rIMENT OF COMMITTEES. The appointment of Parochial Committees was agreed to as follows :—Frodsham, the Parish Council; Frodsham Lordship, the Parish Council; Halton, the Parish Council and Mr. T. Wright; Helsby, the Parish Council; Sutton, Messrs. Jeacock, C. E. Linaker, C. E. Livaker jur., J. E. Bate, A. Lomas and W. Lowe; Stockton Heath, the Parish Council and Messrs. Pilling and Mounfield; Weston, the Parish Council and Mr. J. Chadwick. It was decided that the committees of the Council should be constituted as follows :—Finance Committee, Messrs. Wright, Chadwick, Fletcher, Roylance, Collier, Baxter. Smith, Spruce, Stokes and Guest; Building Committee, the representatives of all districts where the building bye-laws are in force; Highways and Public Health dommittee, all the members of the Coun- cil; Parliamentary Committee, ,Messrs. Woodward, Pilling, Chadwick, Mounfield, Wright, Spruce, Linaker, Roylance, Collier, Lewis, Talbot, Smith, Whitley, Broome, Bolshaw, Henry Davies, and C. E. Linaker, jun., ; Smallpox Hospital Committee, Messrs. Moan- field, Roylance, Broome, Jones; Isolation Hospital Committee, Messrs. Woodward, Pilling, Harrison, Chad- wick, Linaker, Wright, Talbot, Bolshaw, Nfounfield and Guest; Moore Sewerage Committee, Messrs. C. E. Linaker, Jones, Roylance, Mawdesley, Pilling, Baxter, Antwis, Billington and Hallows. Mr. Linaker emphasised the importance of the duties of the last-named committee, pointing out that at an early date something might be forced upon the town- ship of Moore in regard to the disposal of sewage. It would be a very serious thing to carry out a com- plete sewerage scheme, and perhaps the committee might be able to devise some other means of dealing with the matter. Mr. Wright was re-elected chairman of the Finance Committee, and Mr. Chadwick vice-chairman. THE MEDICAL orricEs, OF HEALTH. The Local Government Board wrote sanctioning the re-appointment of Dr. .1. Adams as Medical Officer of Health at a salary of £l5O per annum. FRESTON-O-TH'-HILL SUGGESTION. Mr. John Whitlow, clerk to the Parish Council of Pieston-o-th'-Hill, wrote stating that hie Council had passed a resolution asking the District Council to call the attention of the road surveyor to a length of pave- ment on the road leading from Preston-o-th'-iiill to Dutton which they were a opinion, required re-laying with macadam, Mr. Swinton stated that he bad provided for this impngtrient ig the estimatee for the year. Eargdil ANI) 6oglttAc' ToAg.-1 411fSVON OF D AM AGE. The Clerk reported that the surveyor's certificate of the amount of damage alleged to have been done by Messrs. Jowett Bros to the roads at Kingsley and Norlev during the carrying out of work for the Liver. pool Corporation Water Committee,bad been forwarded to that firm. In reply, Messrs. Jowett stated that the roads were in a very bad state of repair before they commenced work. The Surveyor had taken the oppor- tunity of putting them into thorough repair. a very considerable length having been entirely newly made. The contractors could not hold themselves responsible for the re-making of the Council's roads, but they were

willing to pay what they considered ample compensation for all damage done by them, viz., .£45. The Surveyor's oertificate,stated the Clerk, showed the amounts expended on repairing the road to be 22 lss. and 263115. Bd. He replied to Messrs. Jowett reminding I them that the certificate of the surveyor was for expenditure over and above the usual amount spent by him on the roads in question. They would therefore see how ridiculous was the offer they had made.— Messrs. Jowett wrote him again stating that if he thought the firm must pay accounts, whatever they amounted to, for that class of damage without demur, he was much mistaken. They were willing to pay a reasonable price, and suggested a meeting with repre- sentatives of the Council to arrive at a settlement. The Chairman remarked that as the representative of one of the townships concerned, he considered the offer of £45 was altogether too ridiculous to entertain. The roads were quite sufficient for the ordinary traffic of the district, and in the usual course would not have cost any extraordinary amount to maintain in a satis factory condition. If the firm was not prepared to make a better offer, he suggested that legal proceedings should be taken. The Council instructed the Clerk to press the matter, 'and authorized him to take legal prooeedings if nacos- ary. A STOCKTON HEATH FOOTPATH TO BE CLEARED. The Clerk submitted an application by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. for permission to close a footpath at Stockton Heath, in lieu of which the com- pany proposed to make another footpath almost in the same position, but straighter. The Parish Council had not raised any objection. Mr. Hallows moved that the company's application be granted. The work would be a great improvement to the village, as the present footpath was not safe. Mr. J. Mounfield seconded, and the motion was carried.

PILLMOSS-LANE, WALTON. With reference to the proposed taking over of this lane as a high road repairable by the Council, Messrs. John White, Mclver and Co., Warrington, wrote on behalf of Sir Gilbert Greenall, expressing regret that the Council had been unable to come to any arrange- ment with Mr. James Parr, of Hatton, who was asked to give a piece of land. They were strongly of the opinion that the public ought to bear some share of the cost of making the road, considering the advantage they would derive from it. They asked. the Council to re-consider the subject to see if they could not meet Sir Gilbert with lower terms. The Clerk explained that Sir Gilbert was asked to pay £l5O to give the Council all the stones that were now on the road. If those terms wereaccepted, the Counoil would have to provide £75. Messrs. White, Mclver and Company did not appear to see that the Council was paying anything. It was decided that the clerk should write Messrs. White, Mclver and Company pointing out that the ICouncil would have to incur an expense of £75.

_ HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. Dr. Adams reported that a case of scarlet fever had been notified from Lower Walton. He was informed that measles had become epidemic at Stockton Heath, but as yet he had had no official intimation. Measles were prevalent at Appleton and he recommended that the schools be closed for a further period of two weeks. The report was adopted. HELSBY SEWERAGE SC HEM E.-TH F: ALLEGED DEFECTS. Mr. Linakar reported that the Helsby Sewerage Com- mittee had met at Helsby and made a very careful inspection, as far as they possibly could, of the .sewage works. They had held another meeting that morning, but they did not feel justified in expressing their opinion on the work as it had been carried out. They were, however, so convinced of the truth of Mr. Guest's allegations, that they recommended the Council to instruct the Clerk to inquire from Mr. James Diggle his terms for inspecting and reporting upon the work. The committee were agreed that there was rc;Om for inquiry, and there were certain things in connection with the scheme which reflected grave discredit on someone. The course suggested by the committee was agreed to.

LIFE SAVED BY A FLAW TN A POICER. At an inquest at Edmonton on Tuesday on Albert William Kempton, aged 53, the evidence showed that deceased rose in the night, and taking a poker in two hands struck his wife a blow on the head, which broke the poker. It had a large flaw in it, which probably saved her life. Subsequentiv the deceased hanged him- self to a beam in the cellar.

TRUSTS.

THEIR ORIGIN AND POWER

THE FREEDOM OF FREE TRADE.

WE know little of Trusts in this country, And we have no desire to increase that know- ledge by practical experience. We are most of us quite content to watch their operation in Germany and America without wishing to see them established in our own country. To a certain extent, of course, there are monopo- lies in Great Britain which press heavily upon the people. Railways, land, water, illumina- tion have all been monopolised in different parts of the country; but the tendency is to hand monopolies over to the public rather than to increase their number. We still have our food, our clothing, our furniture, our houses free from the control of Trusts, and if we are wise we shall not curtail that freedom, but rather seek to increase it. Now, although the danger of Trusts is fully understood in 'Great Britain, it is not always realised so clearly that Protection is the parent of Trusts. Many people are willing to enter- tain the idea of Protection who would oppose with all their power any proposal to establish Trusts on a large scale throughout the United Kingdom; and yet they are part of the same idea, the one being the final outcome of the other. Trusts are but Protection grown to perfection.

What is Protection? To understand the relations between Pro- tection and Trusts, it is necessary to define some of the phrases we use. First of all: What is Protection? The answer may be briefly put as follows: Protection is a system by which the cost of foreign imports is increased beyond their natural price through the imposition of Government charges, whereby the home producers of similar goods are protected against foreign competition. Perhaps it, would be better to give that definition a little more simply. Protection imposes a duty or tax upon goods imported from a foreign country. The result is that any person who wishes to purchase those foreign goods must not only pay for the goods, but the tax as well. This, of course, is a serious hardship to the purchaser, but it is a great advantage to the person who is interested in producing similar goods at home. It protects him against all competition from the outside world, and thereby increases his profits at the expense of his fellow-country- men, who, in most cases, are no longer able to purchase goods from abroad because of the increased price. Having given a definition of Protection, let us now consider the obvious results of the establishment of such a system. 1. Competition is restricted to the home pro- ducers. 2. The home producers are able to secure a monopoly of the home market. 3. The home producers having established a monopoly are able to regulate the prices at which their produce shall be sold. 4. A monopoly can very largely dictate the terms upon which its employees shall be paid. Such are the possible results which may accrue . from the establishment of Protection. Now let us carry the matter a little further, and see the relation of Protection to Trusts.

What pre Tr;ists?

A -TRUST is 01(;',r,n1,n1r9+jon of several and separate, but iruslly similar, industries into one concern. America is the home •of the Trust, and there we see almost every business and industry is connected with some Trust. There they have Steel Trusts, Meat Trusts, Provision Trusts. Tin Trusts, Milk Trusts, Clothing Trusts, and so on tnrougnour, almost every trod() in the States. These Trusts Cali control the wages and hours of the workman. They control the quality of the goods srpplied to the public and the prices the public has to pay. The idea of each Trust is to secure in its special trade a esmplete monopolY, so that the people of the 'United States must .either buy from the Trusts or go without! It will not require any argument to show that once a Trust has secured' this monopoly they have the public at their mercy. It is because they have been successful in securing monopolies that Ainerican Trust magnates have grown -into multi-millionaires. They have grown rich ,because they have been able to extort from the public almost any price they choose to ask.' Now the question naturally arises: How is it that Trusts have flourished in America and Germany, but yet have found little encourage- ment in Great Britain ?

===== 0002601_19040423_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
Cultivating Trusts.

THE answer to the above question is: Trusts seldom exist where there are free imports. Tru-ts require a certain artificial soil before they can flourish; that soil is Protection. It is possible to secure the control of any industry in any one country, but it is next to imrossible to secure the control of any one industry in all parts of the world. Differences of nationality, differences of Conditions of life, differences of methods of production, and differences of trade prevent the amalgamation of any one industry throughout the world. But when it comes to dealing with the industries of a single country, where conditions are similar, the formation of a unton between the manu- facturers or producers of similar goods is not only simple, but one of the natural tendencies of modern times. Free imports are the only safeguard against such a union developing into an organisation for the purpose of exploiting the public needs. So long as we have free imports the markets of the wide world are open to the humblest individual. No Trust has yet been able to corner the world's produce, and so long as ive retain Free Trade in Great Britain it will be impossible for Trusts to control either our food supplies, our clothing our furniture, or anything we are able to import. Under Free Trade we buy goods, whether of home or foreign origin, at the natural price. Under Protection we should have to pay not merely for the goods. but as much more as the Trusts could induce the Government to put on,

Trusts and Governments. - 0E of the inost serious *i.esult3 from f'ro- itection, anti tiip oenßequent creation of Trusts, Is the lifiproper influences which powerful trades bring to bear upon a Governmett. We have seen in Great Britain how the driO: interest has !mined the complete mastery of one great party in the state, and how the present Prime Minlifter must obey its coin mands. Does anybody wish to see a dozen such trade interests seeking to Control cur Government? And yet that would be the inevitable result of Protection.' Each trade would seek to have the duty upon its produce put up as high as possible, irrespective of the beeds of the general public. Corruption of our Parliament would inevitably follow. Trtdes when amalgamated under Trusts would have enormous funds at their disposal, with which they could secure the return of candi- dates favourable to thelr interests. And no trade could, under the circumstances, invest its money more profitably than in securing the imposition of heavy duties against its foreign competitors. We have seen these evil influences at work in America, and we should speedily see them in Great Britain if we abandoned Free Trade.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
A BABY WHO SMOKES AND CHEWS.

Eddie Draudt, of Louisville, a three-year-old boy, is an inveterate smoker, and he chews occasionally. His parents (says a New York telegram to the "Daily Tele- graph ") made an application to the Bench to hauelara confined in an institution for young ptople. The child explained that he bad been smoking for several weeks, some older boys having taught him. The doctor proved that the child's health had suffered from the tobacco habit, and a decision was deferred.

GREAT SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL.

The first meeting of the newly-constituted Parish Council of Great Sankey was held in the schools on Monday evening. There were present: Messrs. G. Thompson, G. Higgs, F. T. Jefferson, W. Furness,Harry Fairclough, W. Taylor, and J., Smith (clerk).

_ _ THE CHAIRMAN. Mr. George Thompson was appointed chairman on the proposition of Mr. Taylor, secondedby Mr. Fairclough and supported by Messrs. Riggs and Furness. In acknowledging the compliment, Mr. Thompson said the majority of the members were new to the Council. He hoped they would get along very com- fortably, and that during the ensuing three years they would be able to do some good work for the benefit o the township.

VICE-CHAIRMAN AND OVERSEERS. Mr. William Taylor was appointed vice-chairman. • Mr. George Thompson and Mr. Thomas Halton were ! the two retiring overseers. Mr. Thompson was re- elected and Mr. a Fairclough was appointed to be his colleague.

THE REPRESENTATIVE ON THE EDUCATION SUB-COMMITTEE.

The Council then proceeded to appoint a represen- tative on the Local Education Sub-committee. The Chairman pointed out that Mr. J. Fulton Smith had been the representativehitherto and he hadattended to the duties very well. It was a committee that re- quired a good deal of attention and it was necessary that a representative of thA Great Sankey Schools should be present at the meetings which were held at Earlestown. The representative paid his own railway expenses. Mr. Smith was prepared to act again if the Council were disposed to appoint him. Mr. Taylor proposed and Mr. Higgs seconded that Mr. Smith be re-appointed and this proposition was unanimously agreed to. The Chairman and Mr. F. Birch were appointed as the Council's representative managers of Sankey schools.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SANITARY ARRANGEAITS.

Mr. Furness suggested the advisability of the Council doing something with regard to the sanitary conditions of Hood-lane district. He understood thatimthing had been done by the Council to meet the objectionable sanitary conditions that existed in that quarter He moved that the Clerk be instructed to prepare a return to be • forwarded to each member of the Council showing the number of houses, the price paid per house for sanitary services, the name of the contractor, and whether the work was satisfactory or otherwise. The Chairman pointed out that at.the meeting of the Rural District Council it was intended to appoint a committee to go into the bye-laws regulating the sani- tary arrangements in all the townships of the Union, I and he dare say that a good deal of what Mr. Furness I was referring to would be discussed, and action taken. If he happened to be appointed on the committee he would take-care that all these sanitary arrangements were looked after. . . _

Mr. Furness thought it would be better if that Coun- cil could suggest some means of dealing with the ques- tion at Sankey, as if a general scheme were adopted, they brought others into the ratepaying area who had no interest in the scheme. If the matter were carefully gone into by them they might be able to suggest some- thing to the Rural Council which would minimize the possible expense and avoid the necessity of imposing a general rate upon everybody. A greatproportion of the people who paid rates were farmers, and as he had said, if they could suggest a scheme that would avoid the imposition of a general rate upon all,it would be better. Mr. Thompson remarked that the farming interest at Sankey was not, perhaps, as large as Mr. Furness would imagine. The gross value of agricultural land and buildings came to a little under £4,000, whereas that of works, railways, canals, houses, &c., amounted to over. £9,000.

Mr. Furness said he was not putting the farming in- I terest against the residential. Mr. Thompson said he was quite prepared to study the farming interest, but still it was not quite right to say, as had been said, that only a farmer could represent Sankey. (Laughter.) The whole argument would not do. He did not think it was fair to say that no one was able to represent Sankey except a farmer. Mr. • Jefferson "We are very ungrateful." (Laughter.) Mr. Fairclough seconded and the proposition was agreed to. BUTTERMILK BRIDGE. Mr. Jefferson suggested that a letter might be written to the Rural Council with reference to the state of the footpath across the fields to Buttermilk Bridge. The I bridge also was very dangerous, and he thought the Rural Council ought to put the work in hand at once. The Warrington Corporation had mended their portion of the bridge, but the Rural Council's half was in a dangerous condition. The clerk observed that they might refer the matter to their representative on the Rural Council (Mr. Thompson). Mr. Furness thought matters such as that should be endorsed by the Parish Council, and that the moral support of the Council should be given to their repre- sentative. Mr. Jefferson's suggestion was unanimously agreed to. I

itai yip THE SANATORIUM:4I,VA " Mr. Furness said he had another suggestion to make —that a committee of the Council visit the Sanatorium and inspect the method 4::.f dealing with the sanitaticm, and also visit Winwick Asylum to see the system in operation there. He understood the Winwick system was the most modern, and perhaps they could get the method adopted at Great Sankey and so minimize any danger there might be with regard to the brook. His own opinion was that it was not perfect. Mr. Thompson remarked that the Local Government Board Inspector, guaranteed that the system of sewage was abolutely perfect and said they need fear nothing. He (Mr. Thompson) had had no need to doubt that, and no complaint whatever had ever been made with refer- ence to it. That Council, from time to time, had kept in touch- with the matter and reports of their past meetings would show that such matters were kept to the front. Personally, he felt very strongly on all sanitary aarangements, and thought they ought to be as perfect as possible. Mr. Furness said he did not doubt what the inspector had said, but at the same time he thought that as a Council they onght to be perfectly satisfied themselves that they had-absolutely the best method at the Sana- torium of dealing with sanitation. The Clerk: Presuming it was found that the Winwick system was superior what would be done? Have you any legal right to enforce the Corporation to adopt it against the present one? Mr. Furness: We have the privilege to bring moral influence to bear if not legal power. Mr. Jefferson could not see what better they could do after the inspector, who was an expert, had given them his opinion that the system at the Sanatorium was perfect. Mr. Furness: But I don't think we have anything to lose by carrying out ray suggestion. It is our absolute duty, I think, for us to be mdivic ually satisfied. Mr. Taylor said that samples had been taken from the pipe from the Sanatorium and analysed at the request of one or two farmers whose cattle used the brook, and the report was that it was perfectly free from pollution, and that samples would again be taken from time to time. Mr. Higgs said he took it that the Local Government Board inspector was an expert on the question, and in the, face of his opinion and the result of the analysis referred to by Mr. Taylor, he did not see it was neces- sary to inspect the systems. Even if they did inspect them they were not qualified to say anything about i t,as they were not experts in sanitary matters. They would want someone to advise them. The Chairman : We were told it was absolutely per- fect. Mr. Jefferson did not see what use it would be to make the visits. The Chairman : I think it would be better to leave the matter alone for the present. The matter dropped.

According to an old ordnance map of the district these cottages adjoined and abutted on London road which was then considerably wider than it is now. DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MRS. J. H. CLARKE.— General regret was felt in the village at the painfully sudden death of Mrs. Clarke.wife of Mr. J.H. Clarke,the Chairman of the Stockton Heath Parish Council, who passed away at Heathdale, Grappenhall-road, on Sunday evening. For some time the deceased lady had suffered from henrt affection, but there was nothing to indicate that the end was so near. She was out of doors as usual during the last 'yeek, and on Thursday visited Liverpool. The following evening she became seriously ill,and never regained consciousness. Apoplexy was the cause of death. Mrs. Clarke was much respected and the deepest sympathy is expressed with the husband and three daughters in their bereavement.—The funeral took place at St. Thonias's Churchyard on Wednesday, the Rev. H. F. Pigot officiating. Many relatives and friends attended, the chief mourners being Mr. J. H. Clarke, Mr: Percival. of West Derby, Mr. John Clarke, Mr. Haviour, Mr. Williams (Liverpool), Mr. William Wallington, Mr. Harry Haviour and Mr. Arthur Taylor. Beautiful floral tributes were forwarded by Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall, Mr. and Mrs. Vere B. Davies, and the Wilderspool office staff, as well as by numerous relatives and friends.

Latchford Primitive Methodist Mission.

The annual distribution of prizes in connection with the Latchford Primitive Methodist Sunday school took place on Sunday afternoon. Miss Crampton presented the prizes, and expressed the pleasure it afforded her to be present. Mr. J. Griffiths, who presided, gave an address to the scholars.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church.

The children sang special hymns for the occasion exceedingly well. Services for the People. The Royal CourtTheatre was crowded to excess on Sun- day when the Rev. Herbert Dunnico delivered the first of two sermons on 'What is Christianity is it true "? The preacher appealed to his audience at the outset to rid their minds of all prejudice either for or against. Some of his opinions would clash as much with the views of the orthodox as others would with the opinions of the beterdox.fAll that he asked was that they would give his arguments afair consideration. There were large numbers of people who supposed that modern science and modern criticism had destroyed the foundations of the Christian Faith and who could not understan d how intelligent men could believe in Christianity, but that was due to ' the fact that many men who contemptuously passed judgment upon it had never taken trouble to,form an independent opinion as to what Christianity actually was. They accepted the crude notions of the market place, and then they brought all the forces of scienqe and philosophy to demolish these men of straw and then told us that Christianity stood discredited. The one essential and indispensable condition of passing judg- ment upon Christianity was to know what it was and to know that they must know who Christ was, and before they demolished the Christian faith they must destroy its author. After speaking at some length upon the historicity of Jesus and the historical value of the New Testa,ment,docum.mts the preacher quoted Taeitus,Jose- phus,Pliny, Renan and others in support of his con- tentions. No fair-minded man, he stated, could deny that Jesus was a genuine historical person, and that• in the first three gospels we had a substantially accurate account of His life and work. i With regard to the teaching of Christianity the preacher stated that he taught no creed, no dogma. He did not say believe this or that. He said believe in Me, and. that was the sum and substance of Christianity Christianity was a life; a method of livingit could only be verified in life and a saint was its best apologetic. He frankly admitted that much which had been put forth as Christianity was not Christianity. The creeis and dogmas of the churches had tended to obscure the Christ but "their little systems had their day" and ceased to be, but Christianity remained from age to age to save. In an earnest appeal the preacher pleaded with his audience not to hold back from Christianity because of some intellectual difficulty. It mattered little what we thought about Christ compared with what we were for Him.—The anthem "Abide with me" was rendered in an admirable manner.

POSTHUMOUS CHILD'S SHARE

Seated one day in his arm-chair, feeling his end near, William Lucas, coal dealer, ll „Barley-lane, Blackburn, left all his worldly goods to his wife and "seven chil- dren." He was his own lawyer, and he did not foresee that a posthumous child, Margaret Ellen, was to be born to him. At Liverpool, yesterday, Vice-Chancellor Hall had to decide whether the eighth child was en- titled to a share of the estate. His Honour construed the will as a popularly made one, said he had no doubt the testator intended to benefit all his family, and ordered Margaret Ellen to share alike with the *rest.

GOLF FROM THE ROOF.

There was a remarkable incident in the annual golf match between Oxford and Cambridge Universities played on the Woking links on Wednesday. Mr. C. H. Alison, of Oxford, approaching the last hole in the morning, landed his ball on the top of the club house, where it remained. A ladder having been procured, he climbed to the roof, and from there played a beautiful shot, which enabled him to halve the hole in five.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 23, 1904.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
D HIS FLAGSHIPS

ADMIRAL MAKAROFF

IHE RUSSIAN DISASTER AT PORT ARMOR.

Point by point the Japanese are piling up a terribly long score against the Russians in the Far East: but all blows which they have struck are comparatively insignificant before the blowing up of the Petropavlovsk, Admiral Makaroff's flagship, and its loss with practically all hands, including the great admiral himself, and his splendid staff of experts only second to himself. At least six hundred perished at this one blow, including Verestchagin, the famous Russian battle-painter; and the Grand Duke Cyril, the Tsar's first cousin, was badly wounded, escaping only by a marvellous accident. Above all, the blow to Russian prestige involved in the loss of the Admiral-in-Chief and his flagship is enormous. Naturally, the whole of Russia has been thrown into the deepest mourning, and the Tsar personally is feeling the position very acutely. At first It was obscure how the disaster had occurred, but now it seems that it was due to a very wily piece of strategy upon Admiral Togo's part. As the Russian fleet left Port Arthur, under cover of darkness he despatched some of his smaller vessels to lay some floating mines to impede their retreat into harbour. 'thus, when Admiral Makaroff found the Japanese fleet in overwhelming force and decided to retire, he ran right into Admiral Togo's trap with fatal results. In addition, the Pobieda was torpedoed, and the Bez- strachin sunk; and, though the latter was got safely back into harbour there is little doubt that she has been put out of action for the rest of the war. Thus the fleet at Port. Arthur, now under the command of Admiral Skrydloff, is a mere remnant, and of little fighting value, and it looks as though

thegreat fortress must soon fall before Admiral Togo's repeated assaults. Admiral Ma k- axoff's loss is an inestimable one, as he was the most I trusted o ffi c e:r in the Russian navy, and a seaman of great ' enterprise and scientific know- ledge. He had VASILI VERESTCHAGIN. a particu 1 ar 1 y fine record behind hirft. In 1877 he com- manded the weak torpedo flotilla in the Black Sea, and won the coveted St. George's Cross, and half a decade later he was given a command in the Pacific. In 1890 he was promoted and became Inspector-General of Naval Artillery, and introduced many inno- vations, including the capped shell—a British invention now universally used outside our own Navy. Four years later he was ap- pointed to the Meditteranean command and ' went to the Far East when war with Japan seemed imminent, and two years later he was transferred to the Baltic Fleet, which he commanded until he became Naval Prefect of Kronstadt. He was well-known in Eng- land and universally liked. He invented the ice-breaking Ormak, and resided on Tyneside whilst it was built. Admiral Makharoff was a man of fine presence, not unlike Skobeleff, and a very up-to-date sailor; and it is impossible to overrate his loss to his country. Another world-famous victim was Vasili Verestchagin, whose paintings are as familiar in England as in his native land. His great battle pictures an land and sea, and his painting of snow scenes are inimitable; and he was unquestionably one of the greatest geniuses in the world of art. He loved the ' atmosphere of war and tragedy, though _ theoretically loathing war; and he followed many a war in person, reproducing its brutalism in due course from personal observation. His masterpiece was probably his "Pyramid of Skulls, but his series on Napoleon's retreat from Moscow are equally famous. The latter was exhibited at the Grafton Gallery in 1899. In private life he was very popular and beloved everyivhere he went. His death, too, is one of the sad misfortunes of war, but he, like Admiral' Makaroff, died in the atmosphere he loved.

A LIBERAL M.P. AND CHINESE LABOUR.

Mr. A. B. Markham, the Liberal Member for Mansfield, though he has not been in the House of Commons very long, has managed to keep well in the public eye and to make himself very much talked about. Soon after he first became an M.P., it will be remem- berepl that in a debale on the South African

suestiosi.

launched his f am o us tirade

against

financiers

general

Messrs. Wern- her, Belt, and Co. in particu- lar. They 'm- mediately took

steps to insti- tute proceedings for libel, but

were advised

that all state- MR. A. B. MARKHAM, M.P. ments made in

the Houses of

Parliament are privileged. The,' therefore chal- lenged Mr. Markham to repeat his words outside the House, and he did so at a special meeting of constituents. Proceedings were instantly instituted, but were subse- quently withdrawn upon a satisfactory apology being received. Now Mr. Markham has once more been brought into prominence by refusing to support his party on the question of Chinese Labour. His attitude is a very interesting sidelight upon this vexed question, as he has a practical knowledge of the conditions of labour in the mines in the Transvaal after fourteen years of personal experience on the Rand. His contention is that Chinese labour is not intended to dis- place white labour, but to supplement the shortage in Kaffir labour and thus increase the demand for more white labour. He is wholly opposed to any competition between white and Chinese labour, wishing to keep up the price of the former in South Africa to a pound a day and to find work for our unemployed out there. Mr. Markham. after stating his original and interesting views, has placed himself unreservedly in the hands of his committee, but on this point he refuses to yield.

RETIREMENT OF A GREAT FRENCH SINGER.

It will be a great disappoin ment to the many admirers of the great tenor to hear that M. Jean de Reszke has decided to retire from the stage and to give up singing for

===== 0002601_19040423_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
:AN DE RESZKE

and write a new work, he might be tempted to return, but not under present conditions. Thus the greatest tenor of his age retires from what amounts to ennui and dislike of the monotony of playing the same parts year after year. But M. de Reszke has always had a natural bent for teaching, and his wish is now to utilise his powers by teaching others to sing. He intends, there- fore, to form a little class, a coterie of promising musicians, at his Parisian home in the Arc de Triomphe, to which is attached a small theatre, and there to educate their voices for grand opera. Money not being his main object, M. Jean de Reszke will refuse to take any pupil unless he considers that he or she shows possibilities of becoming a great artist, and there will be two scales of fees, based upon the principle of the rich paying for the poor.

THE CAMBRIDGESHIRE TRAGEDY.

"1 did it for Queenie's sake. She could not be brought up to the life we had led for the last few years." That was how Frank Rogers, a boy of fifteen, the son of a London solicitor, told his sister he had murdered his mother. The case is one of the most pitiful that has ever been enquired into

On Tuesday night (the 12th inst." after supper, the boy Frank entered the drawing - room, where his sister was playing the piano, and going up to her said, " I have shot her ; I think it is for the best." A doctor was summoned and found Mn. Rodgers on the floor of the hall, at the foot of the sofa, with her head resting on it. She was then dead. It is stated that the lad shot his mother with a revolver belonging to. his elder brother, and the cause of the crime was stated at the inquest to have been the accused's irritation at the mother having. given way to drink. The boy. was exceedingly fond of his younger sister, Queerne, and to save her, according to his disordered reasoning, he resorted to the terrible act of taking his mother's life. The accused, wearing his school cap With the crest on it, attended .the inquest. He is a fairly well proportioned lad of medium height with almost black hair; which he wore low over a receding forehead. He gave the impression of being of a sullen and morose disposition. The principal witness was the boy's elder sister, Miss Winifred Rodgers, to whom he made the explanation of the murder given above. " When he said he had done it for Queenie's sake did you under- stand what he meant ? " asked the coroner. Tears dimmed the eyes of the girl as sle replied, "Yes. For the last few years we have been unhappy because my mother had given way to drink." The jury found a verdict of " wilful murder" against the son, whose trial will take place in due course.

DEATH OF A FAMOUS AUTHOR.

Dr. Samuel Smiles, the author of "Self Help," and many other stimulating and interesting works, died at his residence in London on Saturday last at the advanco age of 91. He studied medicine as a pro-. fession and practised at Haddington, N. 8., his birth - place until I 8 38, when his first book entitled "Physical Education; the Training and Management of Children," was published. During his early days he wrote for the news- papers, lectured on chemistry, physiology, and other subjects. At this time his great work,"Self H e 1 p," was pigeon-holed lin his desk await-

R. SMILES.

ing a publisher, and it has only after futile attempts to get the manuscript accepted that Dr. Smiles took the bbld step of publishing it himself at his own expense. As is well-known, it proved an immediate success, and up to the present time upwards of 250,000 copies have been sold. It is interesting to recall that Dr. Smiles was for twelve years, dating from 1859, the secretary of the South Eastern Railway, during which time, Charing Cross Station was built. lit this employment he over-worked himself. and in 1871 was smitten with paralysis, and only complete rest ex- tended over many years, restored him to health and vigour. The veteran economist has lived for many years at Pembroke Gardens, Kensington. He quite recently suffered a very sad bereavement in the death of his son, who dropped dead while dressing for a game of golf. The interment of the deceased author took place at Brompton Cemetery last Tuesday,

WARRINGTON AND BRADFORD DRAW.

Warrington had everything in their favour for the semi-final tie with Bradford on Saturday. The ground, at Broughton was one they knew well, the spectators were mostly 'Lancashire men, and the weather of the sort that Warrington had been hoping for all tke week. In fact the sun shone with a splendour quite foreign to Broughton, and spectators rolled up in their thousands. Many of them jibbed when they found is. as the lowest, price for admission and refused to pay. Still there were some 13,000 present,and the gatem oney £477 15s. 3d. Quite four thousand enthusiasts from Warrington attended, while several hundred accompanied the Brad- fordians. The Warrington team was at full strength, while the visitors had only one absentee—but an important one—in Marsden, the Internationathalf-back, his place being taken by Brear. Some credit is due to the Broughton officials for the care they had taken of the ground, and though devoid of grass it was in excel- lent playing order. Teams and officials : Bradford—Gum', back; Dechan, Mosby, Heseltine, and Dunbavin, three-quarter backs; Surman and Brear, half backs ; Broadley, Hutt, Laidlaw. Feather, F. Smales, D. Rees, T. Upton and Turner, forwards. Warrington—Hallam, back; Harris, Dickenson, Isher- wood, and Fish, three-quarter backs; Davies and Hockenhall, half backs; Thomas, Boardman Morrison, Naylor, Jolley, Lunt, Edmondson and Cook, forwards. Referee, Mr. F. Renton, Hunslet. Touch judges, Messrs. J. H. Morton, St. Helens, and W. Jones, Run- corn. Warrington won the toss,a fact which was duly noted by the crowd, who cheered encouragingly, and Broadley started for Bradford against the wind. Play soon settled at centre, and Bradford were the first to get possession, but Hockenhall downed Surman with promptitude. Bradford were early penalised, but Mos- by returned Fish's kick with such effect that little , advantage fell to Warrington. Good work by the Brad- ford backs carried play into the Warrington half, but Fish and Harris with a couple of brilliant runs and , tackles of Dechan and Dunbavin put Warrington on the I offensive. The attack was hotly pressed, and Davies got away cleverly only to lose the ball when about to pass. Bradford retaliated with passing, but Dicken- son charged Mosby over before he could get going, and cheering was loud and long as War- rington tried the Bradford defence in all direc- tions. Dickenson cleverly punted across, Gunn and Fish having a race for the ball. Both missed it by a trifle and Mosby luckily saved a yard or two from the line. The Bradford forwards rushed to safer quarters, Isherwood finely tackling Laidlaw. Smart passing by Warrington was next seen, but Mosb ac- counted for Isherwood. Then for "four deep," the IBradford men wore penalised again and Fish tried for goal, failing by a narrow margin. Dechan and Mosby ran the ball out again the former being bundled in I touch at centre by Hallam and Fish. The latter soon secured and with a beautiful punt sent the ball in touch near the line. Excitetnent ran high as Warring- ton got possession Davies dashed between the Bradford halves and when challenged he threw to Harris who just beat Gunn at the corner, the first point coming to Warrington after about 25 minutes' play. It was a fine effort by the two men concerned, and though Fish again missed the goal by a trifle, the Warrington supporters were on good terms with themselves. Bradford could make little headway, but they had a short rest while Lunt was brought round from the effects of charging Gunn. Off-side by Boardman Ilost his side a good 30 yards, but smart passing, running , and punting soon had them attacking once more, Fish I only just failing to get up in time for a cross-kick. Bradford were again penalised, and Dickenson had the hardest of luck with a kick for goal, the ball passing an inch or two outside the near upright. Davies. Dickenson and Harris increased the excitement by a run down touch. Again Warrington secured, and beauti- ful combination by Hockenhall, Dickenson and Isher- wood should have resulted in a score but the captain de- layed his transfer to Fish a second too long and was brought down by Mosby. A prompt pass would almost certainly have put the left-winger in. After such a , lively spell as this, the Bradford men succeeded in I changing the venue. Fine passing by Brear, Mosby, Heseltine and Dechan nearly resulted in Warrington's downfall,Hockenhall tackling Heseltine a yard from the line. Warrington were hard pressed till Thomas relieved by a great effort which settled play at centre. A penalty to the Yorkshire men drove Warrington back again and from a scramble near the line Turner made a clever effort td dribble over, Hallam saving miraculously. A penalty to Warrington gave welcome relief, and half- time then arrived with the score: Warrington, one try; Bradford, nil. Dickenson restarted amidst great excitement, and a huge punt by Mosby landed the ball well in the War- rington half. Bradford soon showed they meant busi- ness, and rusLed the first three scrums in irresistible style. A grand position was obtained and some quick passing by the Bradford backs left Harris between Mosby and Dunbavin, the wing man dashing over three minutes from the restart, amid manifestations of de- light from the Bradfordians. Mosby took great pains with his kick at goal but made a poor attempt, and the team remained on even terms. Warrington played up gamely and Fish gained a footing in the Bradford half by a long touch kick. A momentlater he made a brilliant run through the thick of the visitors, dodging man after man in a style which the crowd warmly appreciated. Mosby's ponderous kicking soon drove Warrington back. The Bradford men passed out but they were too well watched to get far, and a dash down the left by Fish again cleared the line. A cross-kick by him resulted in Dunbavin being captured near the line and losing the ball, Edmondson was presented with a grand chance but intrying to pick up 3 yards from the linehe was tackled. by Turner. Bradford relieved with a tremendous for- ward rush, which Hallam checked pluckily. Warring- ton were driven back, and foolish play by Dickenson who ran the ball out from behind the posts nearly brought about Warrington's downfall. Scrummages followed close to the line and Sharratt got over but was recalled. Hockenhall relieved with a neat kick to touch. Smart work by the same player put Warrington on the offensiveagain, Dickenson kick- ing across for Fish who was just beaten by the pace of tbe ball. The game quickly travelled to the other end, Dechan sprinting past Fish with a powerful effort, but being ultimately brought down by Hallam. The final whistle sounded with the game drawn, each side having scored a try.

Fish also added a goal. From this point the Warringtonians had matters practically all their own way. After a scrimmage near the line, Davies had a walk over, and Fish added the extra points. Harris made another attempt, but was held up near the rut. Fish and Isherwood were once more prominent, and Fish, making a brilliant run, scored a beautiful try. , The goal kick by the same player was abortive. The remainder of the game lacked incident, and Warrington ran out winners by two goals, five tries (19 points) to nil.

scored a try and also dropped a goal. Result—Leeds, 10 points ; Batley, 2 points.

HULL v. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS.

On the former's ground, before 12,000 spectators. Carmichael placed a penalty for the Rovers, and Goddard dropped a goal for Hull, the scores being equal at half time. Hull had a kick charged down on resuming, and Fletcher scored an unconverted try. Carmichael kicked a penalty from halfway. The Rovers' forwards con- trolled the game, and Hull seemed well beaten. Result —Rovers,7 points; Hull, 2 points.

WARRINGTON WIN AT LEIGH. Warring6n took their full cup team to Leigh on. Monday, with the exception that Boardman was re- placed by Fairhurst. Leigh also turned out at full strength, before about 1,500 spectators. Teams : Warrington : Hallam; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson,and Harris; Davies and Hockenhall ; Thomas, Edmondson, Jolley, Fairhurst, Naylor, Lunt, Morrison and Cook. Leigh: Pearson ; *Underwood, Messer Bennett, and Neville ; Burgess and Kirk; Boardman, 'Clare, O'Neill, Lee, Kelly, Blackburn, Berry, and Silcock. Dickenson started against a good breeze, and a nice round of passing by the Leigh backs resulted in Neville kicking to Hallam, who was held. Hockenhall soon made headway with a clever run, but Hallam had a kick charged down and Silcock missed a fine chance of scoring. The Warrington forwards then got going with a dribble which traversed half the length of the field. The advantage was pressed home by Davies, Dickenson and Harris combining, the wing man only being tackled on the line. Burgess relieved with a long kick, and Dickenson mulling, Berry had an opening but was teo slow. Warrington were hard pressed until Burgess made a very weak drop at goal. From a penalty a minute later however, the same player placed It good goal against his old comrades. Warrington retaliated with a beautiful round of passing, Fish running to Pearson and kicking over the full-back's head only to knock-on in re-gathering. Latchford Without Parish Council.

The annual meeting of the Latchford Without Parish Council was held at the house of Alderman James Vairdough,Latchford,on Monday evening. There were present :—Aldermen Fairclough and Pierpoint, Messrs. R. T. Fairclough, W. Roberts, S. Shakeshaft, and G. Pilling (clerk).—Mr. R. T. CHRISTIANITY AND BUSINESS.

i The Judge's Gomment in a Warrington

Action.

Before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C., the Warrington County Court on Thursday, Thomas Head, 5, Mill-lane, brought an action against Henry Wardman, 7, Percy-street, to recover £4, balance of purchase money for a business and money lent. There was a counter-claim against the plaintiff, for £lO damages for alleged misrepresentation of the value of the busi- ness.—Mr. T. S. Steel appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. A. Browne defended.

Mr. Steel stated that up to July last, plaintiff carried on business in Lovely-lane as a provision dealer. In consequence of the ill-health of his wife he decided to dispose of the business, and de- fendant expressed a wish to acquire it. After some negotiating plaintiff agreed to sell the business for £lB. Defendant paid £l5 and made arrangements to clear off the balance. Plaintiff also lent the defendant £l. As the £4 was not forthcoming it was decided to take proceedings, and not until then was there any suggestion of misrepresentation of the value of the business.

Plaintiff's wife gave evidence and said she told the defendant that the profit en the business was from £1 to 255. per week. She denied having told several persons that she could not make the shop pay. Mark Johnson 22, Lloyd street, stated that he valued the stock at Rl9, before the sale took place. The stock was sold along with the business and was included in the purchase price of £lB. Defendant was then called and said Mrs. Head told him that the profits were from 255. to 265. per week. When witness took over the business he found that instead of making a profit, he had a difficulty in clearing expenses. Asked why he did not claim damages immediately he discovered that the business did not pay, defendant said plaintiff's wife was a personal friend. She was a pro- fessing Christian like himself, and he did not wish to expose the transaction. His Honour: As I have said in this Court before Christianity is a magnificent thing and business is a magnificent thing ; but as a rule they should be kept in separate ledgers. Defendant admitted that he had had no previous ex- perience of the provision business, before opening the shop. His Honour laid stress upon the defendant not having complained of misrepresentation before the action was brought, and gave judgment for the plaintiff on claim and counter-claim with costs.

MOTOR-CYCLIST KILLED.

Mr. Shirley Price, a well-known resident of Becken- ham, was riding a motcir-cycle along Albemarle-road, in that suburb, on Wedne.day, when he collided violently with a butcher's cart. .He died soon after admission to Beckenham Cottage Hospital.

Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN was congratulated on Tuesday on his courage, and it is certainly fair to say that he has framed his Budget on lines which commend themselves to experi- •enced financiers. A deficit has to be in et, and this may be the best way for a Unionist Govern- ment to meet it. The Budget is free from any fiscal heresies, though the founder of the Tariff Reform League may contemplate with satisfaction the extra twopence on tea, which of course he will have the pleasure of remitting under his great scheme for cheapening the cost of living by Pro- tection. At any rate, the rise in the price of tea bring home to many householders the benefits they already owe to Unionist finance. Whether these will make them crave for the elder Mr. 'CHAMBERLAIN'S panacea of making every mortal thing dearer is rather doubtful.

THE new Licensing THE LICENSING Bill is not the grotesque BILL. measure foreshadowed

by the "Standard" the other day. There is no such crazy proposal as the allocation of the spirit duties for the purpose of compensation for extinguished licences; but the actual measure, should it pass, might be very well entitled an Act to increase enormously the value of public-house property. The holder of a license renewable annually has under the present law no vested interest. By the Bill,his annual leasehold as Sir WILFRID LA.WZON said,onWednesday,will be turned into a freehold. He is a freeholder, who except for misconduct, cannotsbe ejected without compensation. Naturally the value of his property is proportionately enhanced. The Government, in brief,are giving a prodigious bonus to their best, friends. To make it all the more striking,they are providing elaborate machinery for tne administra- tion of the compensation fund by public bodies. Tee money is to be clatributed,it is true,y the liquor trade in the shape of graduated duties on licensed premises. But, in order that the deserv- ing publican should not be inconvenienced by any delay in the collection of these duties, quarter -sessions and county boroughs are to be empowered to borrow money on the security of the annual contribution of licensed promises in their respec- tive districts. A new branch of local government is to be created for the benefit of the brewers. Well might Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN ask why the State should exercise this benevolent guardianship in the interests of the brewers instead oI teaching them to arrange the amount and the manner of their own co npensation among them-. selves ? Tais is a very important point, for the Bill not only gives the annual license-holaier a vested interest ; it also makes quarter sessions his trustees. Tne local lic..ensing magistrates are to have no right to terminate his license on th 3 ground of public policy. If they think this course should be taken,they must state their reasons, and then the case must be reported to the Quarter 'Sessions. rhat authority will decide it, and if the license should be extinguished, Quarter Sessions Will at once take charge of th 3 bereaved, and see that he has his share of the compensation. Quarter Sessions, in a word, is to play the part of guide, philosopher, friend, benevolent uncle, and legal adviser all in one, for the advantage of the most privileged tradesmen. The local licensing magistrates are still to control the publican in all matters relating to the conduct of his business. They can take away his license, if a serious charge against him is, in their judgment, made out; but they are not to have the authority to extinguish his license simply on the principle that licences are too numerous in a given area, and must be reduced. That is professedly the principle of the Bill. Mr' AKERS DOUGLAS says that all parties are agreed on the policy of reducing licenses, but he will not trust the local licensing magistrates to carry it out. Evidently they are not deemed sufficiently considerate to the interests of the trade. The case must be referred to a body which is not on the spot, and is, therefore presumed to be free from bias. We question whether the licensing justices will agree with the HOME SECRETARY. We shall .be surprised if the men most intimately concerned with the administration of the licensing laws should accept this Bill as likely to bring about a substantial reduction of licenses, and to promote the cause of temperance. The gain to temperance is most problematical,but the gain to the licensed victuallers if the remarkable measure should become law, is certain, and solid.

The effect of the Bill in Warrington will be to deprive the local magistrates of their power to refuse the renewal of a licence on the ground of •redundancy or to grant a new licence. These cases will all have to go to Quarter Sessions, where, for .new licenses, the unholy practice of bartering will be warmly encouraged. A cash bribe will not even be refused, for the money can be turned over to the compensation fund. Truly a deplorable state of things if it ever comes to pass! A special clause confers on County Boronghs and Cities the same powers as the .Quarter Sessions will possess—if they have a Recorder or Stipendiary. The fact that County Boroughs such as Warring- ton get along without this highly-paid official will, we are informed, preclude the magistrates from exercising the powers they have hitherto held in the control of the trade. Quarter Sessions steps in again, and the J.P.'s with their valuable know- ledge of the needs and conditions of the district become mere cyphers. No wonder the volume of 9pinion against the Bill here and elsewhere is grow- ing like the national debt. The more one considers it, and the more plainly it hecome3 apparent that the Government has truckled to the trade, which stands to gain everything from the Bill, and the general public nothing. In fact, at the rate things are progressing—or, we should sAy retrogressing—it will not be long before the brewer and the publican stand on a level footing with the State and dictate what they consider are the best measures for the welfare of the nation and themselves.

A commendable effort is being made to organise the Liberal forces in Warrington in view of a .general election. The election may not be Imminent but it cannot be much longer delayed, and it is well to be ready for any contingency. rho Liberal workers in Whitecross and St. Austin's Wards gathered in strong force on Wednesday evening, and after appointing the officers and committees of the respective wards they had the Pleasure of listening to a well-reasoned address by Mr. A. H. Crosfield on the political situation. Mr, erosfield was in good form and in exposing the retrograde policy of the Government, he pointed out that any. one of the topics before the electors at the present time was suffi- cient to rally all progressive forces to one co,n .812 eon standard. In Warrington there are welcome Iglus of a re-awakening of Liberal enthusiasm, and With a united front to the enemy,Warrington may

jThe Warrington Rural i District Council means ' to have for its Chairman whomsoever it likes, the [ electors or anybody else notwithstanding. Last year Mr. Thomas Halton filled that responsible office, but at the recent elections "ungrateful Sankey" failed to return him at the head of the poll, and he therefore ceased to be a member of the Council. On Wednesday, the Councillors showed a touching measure of attachment to their late chief, and calmly co-opted him to the Council and re-appointed him to the chair. There was some opposition, it is true to this unusual procedure, but the magnanimous ones had their own sweet way. It was argued, on the other hand, that the legislature did not contem- plate the elevation of defeated candidates to the chairmanship of local authorities and this view is, we consider, the correct one. T(1(1 Rural Council created rather a dangerous precedent in thus stretching the interpretation of the law to its own ,atisfaction. Mr. Halton is indeed a lucky man, and the chairmanship under such unique conditions ought to be a doubly-prized honour.

It is to the credit of Warrington that it is tile first municipality in the United Kingdom to adopt byelaws under the Employment of Children Act. The Home Secretary (Mr. Akers-Douglas) made the announcement in the House of Commons that Warrington is the only instance where the terms of the byelaws have been provisionally settled, but in about twenty-four other places the byelaws are under consideration. This distinction which has fallen to our local legislators is in a large measure due to the commendable energy displayed by the Town Clerk in framing the byelaws. It is, of course, one thing to frame byelaws for the protec- tion of child life and quite another to see that they are carried into effect. In Warrington, as elsewhere, many abuses and objectionable practice have fallowed in the wake of child labour, and it is with a view of preventing them in the future that Parliament has passed the Employment of Children Act. The success or failure of the Act must depend largely upon the administratiou o f its provisions, arid there is reason to believe that the employers effected in Warrington are tvilliwr to carry out the byelaws, if not to the letter, at- least in the spirit in which they have been con- ceived. "Esperanto" sounds like the title of a new comic opera, and probably the bulk of our readers know so little about it that they would believe it was if we made an assertion to this effect in all seriousness. Really, " Esperanto " is something much more importance.lt is the title of a compound, vet simplified language, invented by a foreigner for lise by all the peoptes of the earth. The advant- ages of a common langnage are so obvious as to need no recapitulation, and Mr. H. P. Hughes' suggestion in our correspondence column merits more than passing consideration, and students of languages at any rate should welcome an oppor- tunity of adding the newest to their list. Mr. Hughes scarcely uses the correct phrase when he says "Esperanto is the name of the most perfect attempt to provide mankind with a second lang- uage." So far mankind has not got one language, hut a multitude of languages. The word should, we would suggest, be common, but it is probably only a slip of the pen on Mr. Hughes' part. The study of Esperanto, it is interesting to learn, is making real and rapid progress. 'We shall be glad to hear how Mr. Hughes' suggestion is received

ocally. Our ever-vernal friend Thomas Knowles has blossomed forth again, as a letter in another column shows. In addition to the letter, Thomas perpetrated a little poetry, but we have spared nur readers that. When he talks about Councillor Dodds still entertaining" some hope of being re- habituated in the public confidence "he misses the mark by a very long way. Councillor Dodds pos- sesses the confidence of the Warrington public to a greater extent than ever before, and this same confidence extends throughout the Conncil Cham- ber' as vas evidenced when Mr. Dodds spoke upon the financial position of the borough. H yvever, Thomas Knowles may not be serious—he has a flippant way of alluding to the mother of Moses .nd would even "impeach "a malady—whatever that may mean. As a joker Thomas is rather heavy, unfortunately, but he seems to hold a watching brief for somebody. Who is it, Thomas? Have you some "friends, brothers and brothers- M-law "with tender skins and easy consciences ?

The name of Warrington is just now rising to the top-most pinnacles'of fame—in a football sense. During the week the local Rugby Club has won outright the handsome trophy put up for competi- tion by the South West Lancashire Clubs, while in the replayed semi-final with Bradford on Wednesday, Warrington emerged triumphant and have thus qualified to meet Halifax in the final for the Northern Union Cup at Salford on Saturday next. This tie represents the sur- vival of the fittest throughout LancaAire, Cheshire, Yorkshire, Westmorland, and Dur- ham and Northumberland so that the veriest tyro can appreciate the magnitude of Warrnigton's performance up-to-date. They have been in the final once before and failed, but we will not consider failure on this occasion. The team, almost composed of local players, has per- formed so well recently that anything other than success is not for a moment anticipated. There will be a fine exodus from Warrington to Salford on Saturday, and if the Cup " comes home "as the saying goes, the scenes in the evening are likely to be something extraordinary. Let us hope that Warrington will be the champions of the North by the end of next week. Honours for the old town have not been as common as hard names in recent years. Play no W Arrinn.f*.nrt

EXHIBITION Al' 'frit nutit HALL. Interesting Attractions. Many Warrington people have been attracted to the Parr Hall during the week by an interesting trades ex- hibition, representative of leading firms, which will con- tinue until Friday evening next, The stalls have been pleasingly arranged, and the edibles which are so tempt- ingly displayed by some of the exhibitors have been freely sampled. Local firms are, as usual, well to the fore. In the entrance hall, Mr. B. Wadsworth, of the Music Depot. 59, Sankey-street, has on view a particularly fine collection of pianos, as well as a beautiful organ by Estey. Particular attention is again drawn to the pianos of John Broadwood and Sons which have gained a world-wide reputation. Every instrument is fully warranted, and is sold at the very lowest possible cash price, although arrangements are made for easy payments at a slight increase on the "prompt cash " price. Messrs. W. H. Ireland and Co., of Great Sankey, have a creditable exhibit of the well-known Walton Cycles, together with a 3 horse power Walton motor bicycle. The machines have all the latest improvements, some being fitted with the Fagan two speed hub which en- ables the rider to surmount hills of a steep gradient with very much greater facility. The pichmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd., show a number of the gas stoves and cookers which are used and appreciated in so many households. A new speciality is the enamelled pastry tablet which should readily commend itself to the housewife. The cooking appliances can be obtained on hire from the Corpora- tion at low rents, and they are fixed free of charge. One of the most artistic stands is that of Messrs. Van Houten. It is in the form of a miniature refresh- ment saloon, perfectly and artistically fitted up. The world-famed Van Houten's Cocoa is served in Royal Worcester Cups, at the nominal charge of one penny, this including a biscuit, and a dainty serviette, in addition to which, ladies desirous of trying the cocoa at home, are readily supplied with a pretty tin con- taining sufficient Van Houten's Cocoa for several cups. Messrs. Van H3uten also exhibit their new eating chocolate which ranks among chocolates as Van Houten's Cocoa does among cocoas; th 6 quality being such that it will undoubtedly attain the same popu- larity that Van Houten's Cocoa now enjoys all over I the world. At another well-arranged stall cups of Bovril are dis- pensed, and visitors may also obtain the company's patent relishes, sauces and soup powders. The Anglo- Swiss Condensed Milk Company, the largest manu- facturers of condensed milk in the world, exhibit their dainty specialities, including the "Milkmaid " and " Ideal " brands of milk. A sample cup of coffee and milk is served for id. Every afternoon and !evening an enjoyable mltsical programme is presented by a ladies' concert party. and a mystifying side-show is given by "the Flying Lady." At intervals there are cookery and laundry lectures. Next Thursday evening there will be a special promenade concert, when the programme will include songs illustrated by limelight dissolving views.

LOCAL NEWS.

Coming Events.

At W3-cliffe Church to-morrow (Sunday), the Sunday School anniversary will be celebrated. The Rev. W. Evans,. of Blackpool will preach morning and even- ing, and there will be special music. Chambers of Imagery" will be the Rev. R. Wilkins Rees' subject at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church to-morrow (Sunday) evening. The annual exhibition of pictorial photographs in connection with the Warrington Photographic Society will be held at the Old Academy from Tuesday to Saturday next. Admission wil be free each day from 2to 10 p.m. and there will no doubt be numerous visitors to the exhibition which promises to be a very interesting one. Knutsford " Royal" May Thy Festival will be held on Monday, May 2nd. The festival at Lymm will take place on Whit-Thursday, May 20th. A May festival in connection with the Band of Hope Union will be held in the Parr Hall on Thursday and Friday, May sth and 6th, when the cantata " The Band of Hope Queen " will be rendered.

Warrington and Children's Employment.

Replying to Mr. Tennant, who -asked the Home Secretary from which Councils he had received by- laws under the Employment of Children Act, and which of these had received his approval, Mr. Akers- Douglas says : By-laws have been submitted in draft by the London County Council, the City of London,and the authorities of about twenty-four other places, and are now under consideration. In one instance—Warrington—the terms of the by-laws have been provisionally settled, and are now awaiting formal adoption and confirmation under the procedure prescribed in section 4 of the Act.

Good Templary. The weekly session of the 1.0.G.T. Temperance Mission Lodge was held on Wednesday in. gbenezer Church, Buttermarket street. There was a good attendance. It was decided to hold a public meeting on May 30th and reproduce" Fast Life."• Bro. C. J. Sheath gave a brief report of the Convention meeting on Saturday at Earlestown. The members indulged in the popular programme, " Question Night." Among the subjects spoken on were Compulsory Military Service, Compensation, Chinese Ifibour, and the duties of a Sunday school secretary. The replies were very interesting and provoked some discussion. Cheshire Lines Platelayers' and Workmen's Provident Society. The 29th annual meeting of the Cheshire Lines Platelayers' and Workmen's Provident Society, which consists of the workmen employed in the Engineer's Department of the Cheshire Lines Railway', was held at the works, Battersby-lane, Warring- ton, on Thursday. The chair was taken by Mr. Harry Blundell, M. Inst., C.E. the Chief Engineer of the Company, and the meeting was largely attended by the officials and delegates from Lancashire and Cheshire. The Secretary sub- mitted the annual report, which stated that the number of members was 1,127. A large sum has been expended in aid of 282 sick members who had received assistance from the funds of the Society, and 32 death claims had also been paid. The repprt and statement of accounts havingbeen passed, Mr. Thomas Pratt was re-elected secretary for the ensuing year, and VlesFs. George Walker and R. Jones were re-elected auditors.

St. Peter's Church Gleaners' Union.

In connection with the above, a social gathering was held in the schoolroom, John-street, on Monday evening last and was well attended. In the absence of the Vicar, who was out of town, the curate, Rev. D. R. James, presided. During the evening an address was delivered by Mr. H. J. Powell, of Liver- pool, hon. secretary of the G-leanors ' Union for the Liverpool Diocese. His remarks were most interesting and instructive. ,great enthusiasm was manifested by the announcement that no less a sum than £30,000 had been raised towards the Funds of the Ohurch Missionary Society by means of the "Million Shilling Fund" recently inaugurated, and that in addition, £20,000 had been raised by 'special donations, making a grand total from these sources alone of £50,0007 thus freeing the Society from what would otherwise have been a serious deficit. This special effort was entirely in addition to the Society's ordinary annual income which is raised §olely by voluntary contributions. A vote of thanks to the speaker was proposed by the Rev. D. R. James and seconded by Mr. Thomas S. Steel. Songs were ably rendered by Miss 0-lover, Miss Hughes, and Mr. Reddish. Miss Stanley's selections on the mandoline were greatly appreciated. Mr. F. Webster acted as accompanist. Light refreshments were served during the evening and great credit is due co the ladies who had the arrangements in hand.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

LIVING ON BREAD AND BUTTER.

At Widnes, on Thursday morning, Thomas M'Gregor, 11, Davies-street, was charged, at the instance of the N.S.P.C.C., with child neglect. Mr. Knowles, who prosecuted, said the children, as well as Mrs. M'Gregor, were kept without sufficient food. They had lived upon bread and butter almost entirely for the last twelve months. Had it not been for the assistance of a son age!' twenty-one, who had done his utmost to keep the home going, things would have gone very bad with the family of five children.—Dr. Hutchinson stated that defendant was delicate, but his health would not deter him from doing light work.—Prisoner was sent ta gaol for two months. RUNCORN URBAN COUNCIL.

STONING A HEN.

At Runcorn PettiKE;;lOnS—on---ifonday,JohnChesters (15) and Richard Malone (17), Mill-brow, Runcorn, were summoned for ill-treating a hen by throwing stones at it on tie 10th inst. They pleaded not guilty.—John Kenwright, Heath-road, said he kept fowls. On Sunday week some of the fowls were at the rear of the house. A little boy told him that some lads had been throwing stones at the hens. He went out and found one of the hens "struggling for life with a broken leg." He spoke to defendants and they laughed.—P.C. Davies said he received a complaint, and saw the boys. Chesters said he was willing to pay half if the other lad would do similarly. He said they both threw stones, but Malone broke the hen's leg. Malone denied this. He admitted that they both threw stones. The lads agreed to pay for the fowl.—There were two previous convictions against Malone. The lads were fined in the costs 6s. 6d. each.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRE AT HAZLEHURST'S.

Early on Sunday morning a are broke out in the new glycerine shed at Hazlehurst's Soap Works, Runcorn, and did damage which is stlted to amount to £1,500. The watchman, George Clarke, made an inspection of the works about half-past four. At five o'clock he was in his cabin, when he neard acrackling noise. He went out and saw that the glycerine shed was on fire. He raised an alarm by sounding the works whistle In a few minutes however, the flames were through the roof of the shed, and when the brigade, under the direction of Superintendent Wright, arrived on the scene, there was no hope of saving the building. In the shed were four large tanks or wooden supports, and the efforts of the brigade and workmen were directed to- wards preventing the destruction of the supports and the fall of the tanks on the machinery. They succeeded in this though some of the beams were half burnt through. The shed was completely destroyed, and the machinery consisting of vapourizers, vacuum pumps,&c, was badly damaged. The damage is covered by insurance. The employment of about a dozen workmen will be affected.

ST. HELENS.

SUICIDE AT THE PUBLIC BATHS._

Suicide was Committed in a startling and determined manner at the St. Helens Corporation Baths on Monday night. At a quarter to seven an old man applied in the usual way for a private bath,and he was shown into one of the rooms and the bath was filled by an attendant, who closed the door. Some time afterwardsiVaLomax, the superintendent of the baths, went along the corridor, and seeing the door of the bath still fast, he opened it, and was horror-stricken to see the bath apparently full of blood. On the water and blood being run off the man was found in the bath with his throat cut. He was quite dead, and had evidently been determined to put an end to his career, for besides the terrible wound in his throat, he had, as strength failed, slipped down into the water, and been drowned. The police and Dr.. Unsworth were called in, but all that could be done was to remove the body to the mortuary. The wound in the throat had beeninflicted by means of a penknife.—At midnight the body was identified as Thomas Blow, aged seventy, of 5, Wycherley-street, Prescot. He was for forty years in the service of Lord Derby, being occupied in the stables, but recently he had been in Lining health and had been an out-patient at the Liverpool Infirmary for an affec- tion of the eyes, and the condition of his sight had caused him great anxiety. He had been depressed in spirits for some time.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LOWTON MINER KILLED.

About seven o'clock on Monday morning, Richard Potts, of Newton-road, Lowton, was working at the bottom of the shaft at No. 4 Pit, Bickershaw Collieries, Leigh, when he was struck on the shoulder by a brick, which knocked his head against an iron plate. The skull was fractured and death instantaneous.
