PENKETH.

WESLEYA.N CHAPEL.— Last Sunday was a special day at the above place of worship. The anniversary services in connection with the choir were held, and a great effort had been made to make the services as attractive as possible. This end we are pleased to say, was attained, and the large congregations at each ser- vice, testified to the excellence of the singing. The morning service was conducted by the Rev. John Gunnell, and the anthem" Oh give thanks unto the Lord" was rendered by the choir. Special hymns were sang,and organ selections were admirably rendered by the organist, Mr. G. W. Weaver. In the after- noon, Mr. James Dodds, presided, and a very pleasing programme of sacred music was gone through. Mr. Frank Hickman was the organist, and he gave the con- gregation much pleasure with his selections. Mrs. A. Booth, sang with much taste and expression the solos. "I will extol Thee 0 God," and "Oh Divine Redeemer," and her singing was much admired. The ckairman also addressed the meeting. Theevening service was largely attended, and the preacher was the Rev. G. H. Carnson. Mr. Levi Richardson, was unable to be present, and in place of his solo, the choir rendered the anthem "Oh worship the King" the solo being taken by Miss Connie Atherton. The services were continued on Monday night when a cantata "Penitence, pardon and peace," was rendered. The meeting was presided over by Mr. David Garnett, who spoke of the early days of Wesleyanism in Penketh and gave some interesting reminiscences of his connec- tion with the choir of the Chapel. • The cantata was a very pretty production, and the singers entered fully into the spirit of their work, the singing being of a very good class. The soloists were Miss C. Atherton, Miss E. Plumpton, and Mr. H. Plumpton. Mr. F. 0. Robinson gave 'a very vigorous rendering of the recita- tive and solo " I feel the Deity. within," and "Arm, arm, ye brave." Mr. Ernest Garnett was prevented from attending, and in place of his violin solos, Mr. Robinson gave another item. The collection realized the sum of .00. . _

LIBERAL CLUB.—The annual meeting of the above club was held on Thursday night last. The President Mr. J. J. Withinshaw presided, and their was a fair attendance of members. The Secretary Mr. F. Wilkin- son presented a report of the year's work,and the club is now in a very healthy state,the balance sheet of Mr. Tiltson the treasurer, showing a good balance inhand. The President was re-elected to the position, and Mr. Qilston also retains the position of treasurer. Mr. F. Wilkinson resigned his position of secretary, and Mr. H. Davies was elected to fill the position. Matters of interest pertaining to the club were discussed and the meeting terminated.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
LAU, RIG [ITS RESERVED.]

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN,

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour and Social Problems."

OUR INDIAN FRONTIER. lam convinced that we can gain nothing, but are pretty sure to loose a great deal,in prestige,ia honour, in the valuable lives of our officers and soldiers, by interfering actively in the affairs of Central Asia,and that, so far from strengthening our tenure of India, we must thus shake it to its very foundations. Nor am I insensible, I admit, to the financial aspects of the question. I know well what are the wants of India; how infinite are the material requirements of the country; how limited is the accumulation of capital; how obnoxious is every description of taxa- tion to all classes of the people.—LonD LAWRENCE. . . .

When our Indian officials Vi;ere provoking our last war with Afghanistan, "Punch" gave us a capital cartoon illustrating the methods by which our "raids," and "peaceful missions," and "punitive expeditions," and "little wars" are organised. The central figure in that cartoon is a bull, with John Bull's head and face and a ring through its nose. Disraeli was Prime Minis- ter at the time, and he is pictured in a ploughman's smock and cap, leading, the bull through dark mountain passes and rocky defiles with a light heart. The Bull says, "I don't like the looks of this, Ben; where are you taking me ?" Disraeli says, "Come along, it is all right; we are going to seek a scientific frontier." The sting of this sarcasm lies in its truthfulness. Our hunting after a "scientific frontier" for India has been the excuse for all the devilry that has been done in our name among Afghans, Burmese, Persians, Pathaus, and all the tribes that separate India from Russia and China. This policy of lying, and thieving, and aggression, and bloodshed is known In India as a "forward policy," and it is naturally advocated by military advisers like Lord Roberts and Lord Kitchener, who get their living by fighting. But it is bitterly opposed by statesmen like Lcrd Lawrence and the Marquis of Ripon, and other enlightened viceroys, who had no axes of their own to grind.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
1.-NATURE HAS GIVEN A SCIENTIFIC FRONTIER TO INDIA.

And it is one of the strongest natural defences in the world. If you will study a good modern map of Central Asia you will find that between India, on the one hand, and Russia and China on the other, Nature has piled up mountain ranges and glaciers, and deserts enough to set up a vast neutral or buffer" territory, that effectu- ally separates them. And this neutral country is peopled by warlike tribes who will not molest us if we let them alone, but they will fight to the death against any nation that tries to subjugate them. All we have ito do is to mind our own business and tarry in our own territory and use the frontier that Nature has provided.

Axe Misfortune is that we have not sense to use it, and our military advisers are doing their level best to weaken and destroy it. If you cross the Indus and go northward out of India you enter the defiles and moun- tain passes inhabited by the Pathans. When you are out of their country you are face to face with some of the highest mountains in the world, that must be crossed by painful climbing, and if you manage to force your way through the awful mountain passes you have then to cross an alpine country as large as France before you get to Russia. It is a land of rock, and snow, and glacier, and morass, and deserts. So you see we have a magnificent defence of desert, mountain and fighting tribesmen.

The restless, warlike,nomadic tribes who inhabit the borderland between India and Russia owe allegiance to nobody. They are free and independent, and they mean to remain free. They elect their own tribal rulers, and manage their own affairs, and though the various tribes are jealous of each other, and are .separated by local feuds, and petty• quarrels, they always bury the hatchet in face of a common foe. They are splendid fighters, or they would never have maintained their in- dependence so long against Russians, Afghans,Persians, Chinese, Sepoys, Sikhs, and English. Now I think I have shown you that Providence has provided for India one of the strongest natural barriers against invasion that can be found in the world. Bat the advocates of this "forward policy" say that we must be prepared for all possible and probable contin- gencies. They ask us to believe that some day another 'Bonaparte, or CEesar, or Alexander the Great will arise and invade India, He will come from Russia, they tell us, and these croaking prophets advocate a "forward policy "to meet an invader who has not yet been born. For the sake of argument, let us suppose that another Alexander should arise and lead against us an army of two million men. He no sooner leaves Russia than his troubles begin. He plunges into a desert, where he will have to depend on Russia for all his supplies. He must carry not merely his own weapons and ammunition, but every morsel of bread and every drop of water. The salt lagoons would not keep a crow alive, and his troops would die like flies of famine, and dysentery, and disease and cold.

But suppose he gets safely over the desert, and enters the mountain passes with the remnant of his broken larmy. He must cut his way through eternal snows, and awful glaciers, and rovky defiles for many weary imonths. He must depend for supplies on Russia still, and every ounce of food and stores must be carried nearly a thousand miles. If he ever lives to cut his way through the passes and come out on the southern side of the mountain ranges he will simply drop out of the frying pan into the fire. The Hindoo Kush mountains are 25,000 feet high, and their southern spurs stretch away to the plain of the Indus. This vast track of mountainous country is inhabited by more than 200,000 'Pathans. They are as brave, hardy, patriotic, and fond of freedom as the Montenegrins, and if ever the promised invader comes they will make mince-meat of him. They would just as soon kill a Russian as an Englishman or a Chinaman, who threatened their liberties, or meddled with their affairs. Every foot of these rocky defiles and wild mountain passes would be fought by these bloodthirsty Pathans, and if ever the new Alex- ander reached India at all it would be a miracle. And if he did cross the Indus then the real fighting would begin. Worn and exhausted, and decimated by his diffi- culties on the way, he would get a warm reception from Sikhs, and Sepoys, and Unglishmen.l But if lam to sit in judgment on this" forward policy," and make up my mind what is best for India and her people, I want to learn wisdom from past experi- ence. Ido not ask what is likely to happen under any conceivable circumstances? I want to know what has happened, and what seeds these military rulers are sow- ing, and then I shall know what sort of harvest to expect.

11.-THE FORWARD POLICY WILL KEEP INDIA IN PERPETUAL WARFARE.

When we first got a footing in India, and established our factories and trading stations there, we behaved ourselves pretty well for awhile. We were making money, and living in good pastures, so we were content to pasture on our own side the hedge. But ambitious fighting men settled there, who had everything to gain and nothing to lose by fighting, because It was their trade. So thos3 mischief makers picked quarrels with their neighbours, and invaded their territories, and annexed their property, till our Empire extended from the Indian Ocean, to the Himalaya Mountains. Every sane and prudent statesman hoped they would cease their wars of aggression when they got there, and spend their energies and their money in developing the territory they had got, instead of fight- ing for more. Lord Lawrence, who is affectionately spoken of as the "Saviour of India" advocated the construction of reservoirs and irrigation works to in- crease the fertility of the soil. He advised the people to build railways, and make good roads, and drain pesti- lence swamps, and fight the Plague and other tropical , diseases by sanitary improvements. His policy was to stop our expenditure on war, and spend our money on wise, efficient, and economical government, and make the native under our rule happy, and prosperous, and contented. But as long as human nature is what it is, military rules will spend money on war, and if they cannot find a decent excuse for warlike operations they will pick a quarrel with somebody for pure cussedness, and let us in for an expensive and inglorious campaign. If I had my way, the military men who organise these campaigns outside our own territories would be tried for their lives, and they would be hanged or shot. Their usual game is to go with a number of armed followers into a strange country on a surveying expedition, to map out the roads and measure the distances. If that does not raise a row,they build a blockhouse or a fort and hoist the British flag. If that does not fetch the natives, they proclaim a Protectorate and proceed to levy taxes. This is usually the last straw that breaks the camel's back, and when the natives wallop the tax- gatherers we accuse them of a "treacherous attack" on us, and we shoot them down, as we butchered the people of Thibet the other 'day. Then we have a fine excuse for mowing them down with our machine guns, and stealing their territory, and appointing the ring- leader of this plot as governor with a few thousand pounds a year and a residence. If he had his deserts he would be hanged and gibbeted as a warning to all other mischief-makers. These rascally raids cost a pile of money. Our raid on Quetta cost over two millions and a half. Our trip to Chitral cost nearly as much, and the Jameson Raid, in South Africa ran up a little bill of tN7vo hundred and fifty millions. 'England is rich and she can afford to play the fool after this fashion occasionally, but India is too poor to follow her example. She has a war every three years, and a famine every four, and plagues and pestilences all the time. Forty thousand of her people are dying every week of the Plague, and she is making no serious efforts to stamp it out. The natives of India are miserably poor, and they have neither voice nor vote in the management of their own affairs. They are governed by aristocratic rulers from England, who receive big salaries for precious little work, and who retire early,on heavy pensions that are squeezed out of the poor taxpayers. Every little war or frontier raid means a fresh addition to the burdens of the people. Every neiV advance requires more money and more troops, at the nation's expense.

Every tribesman we kill is one more Warrior taken out of the way of our coming invader. Every new bridge we build, and every new road we make beyond our own frontier, is so much aid and assistance given to the enemy we are expecting. The "Forward Policy" is El fine thing for officers and soldiers who want speedy pro- motion and higher pay, but it is a disastrous game for those who have to pay the piper. India has suffered from years of misgovernment, and heavy taxation, and war, and plague, and famine. What do the Hindoos think about us? Sir Auckland Colvin said, at the time of the Chitral war, that "the feeling of the native population towards us by no means grows more friendly. Suspicion is taking the place of con- fidence, and our dislike is substituted for good will." If that be true, we are breaking down the moral defences of India in order to alter its physical defences. /f the Hindoos were thoroughly contented with our ' rule, and loved us, and trusted us, we need never fear any foe from outside. But if India becomes sullen and discontented, and rebellious, she may give us very serious trouble' aud the vast empire we won by the sword may be taken from us in the same way. India has been ruled for manycenturies by the sword. She has known no gospel but the power of brute force. Our military advisers and our fightin men ha,ve brought the country to the verge of ruin. It is high time that the Gospel of Jesus Christ should help to shape the destinies of that unhappy empire. Righteousness, and justice, and peace, and truth, and kindness, and common sense, and common honesty are sorely needed in the government of India just now.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE CUP WON.

Great Doings by Warrington.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

By " Ovalist."

With the Sputh West Lancashire Cup secured, and the Northern Union Cup " invoicad' Warrington nay fairly congratulate themselves on having done a good week's work.

Third time pay's for all !—or it is popularly sup- posed to do at any rate. Warrington are for the third time in the running for the Northern Union Cup—the blue ribbon of Northern Union football—and will appear in the final tie against Halifax at Salford next Saturday. Let us hope that this time there will be no slip between the cup and the lip such as there was at Headingley in the 1900-1 season. Then it will be remembered that Warrington had performed exceed- ingly well and were expected to account for Batley all right—but they failed. The conditions are very similar t-i-day, when Warrington are due to engage the present cup-holders, Halifax, who upset the pretensions of Salford so signally a year ago. There is the important difference, however, that Warrington will this time be playing on Lancashire soil, and will possess the sympathy of the crowd; and, moreover, they have the painful experiences of the last final behind the n. They should win, unquestion- ably,though " Old Ebor," with whom I ;had tea on Wednesday at Huddersfield, still pins his faith to Halifax. But then, of course, " Old Elam." .is a Yorkshireman ! He is . welcome to his opinion, especially seeing that he tipped Pontefract and Brad- ford—now numbered among the slain of Warrington.

In discussing the final tie prospects, I must not overlook how Warrington got into the last round. Their defeat of Bradford at Huddersfield was a re- markable and memorable achievement,and one that will never b 3 forgotten by those whowere fortunate enough to see it. Having only succeeded in drawing with the Bradfirdians on Saturday at Broughton the odds were certainly against Warrington in the replay• on Yorkshire soil. But the present Warrington fifteen possess pluck, and they entered the Fartown enclosure with grim determination and just a little quiet confi- dence. By the way, what a picture Fartown was on Wednesday! The turf was in splendid order, green with grass, and the terrace and oncllsure well filled with people. Bradford folks were full of confidence, in fact there was any amount of two to one betting in Yorkshire on the result. I travelled down by car after the match with a canny tyke viho had been offered two to one and had taken Warrington's chance with the result that he was complacently jingling a few sovereigns in his trousers pocket. Very wrong of him, of nurse, but these sporting characters will have their little wager.

Bradford were still without George Marsden, this time owing to the working clause, but they were not compelled to make the disastrous experiment they did, by putting Haseltine half back in place of Brear , and Dunbavin centre, with Sinton on the wing. This was a piece of bad judgment, and as a result, the Bradford back division fell far below Saturday's standard, when it menaced the Warrington line almost the whole of the second half. Dunbavin travelled back with the Warrington team, and was loud in his complaint of the re-arrangement. It had certainly a good deal to do with losing Bradford the match.

On Wednesday,B radford were successful in winning the toss, and Broadley smiled broadly—l am quite serious—when he got his back to the breeze for the opening half. Somehow, he and his men made poor use of it, and though they attacked persistently for two-thirds of this half, they were seldom really dan- gerous. True enough, Warrington had anxious moments. Dunbavin was only tackled a yard out once, and a couple of " scrums " followed on the line amidst terrible excitement. But the Warrington men cleverly cleared. Again, the citadel seemed bound to fall when the Bradford forwards got the ball at their toes with only about ten yards to go. Hallam was there, however, and that was sufficient. He dived between the legs of his opponents with a pluck which fairly roused the crowd, and what is more he secured the ball! That was about the extent of Bradford's attack. How Warrington retaliated is fully told in another column. Fish's try was indeed a gem. The move- ment started at the Warrington quarter and was carried on in perfect style until Isherwood fed his wing,with the defence of the Bradfordians completelY pierced.

Turning round with a lead of three points,Warring- ton always looked winners. They had got the measure of their opponents even in the scrummage, and they completely overplayed them throughout the second half. Fore and aft the work of the team was accomplished with deliglit!al precision, and it was not very long before Dickensnn simply rocketed through the Bradford backs after a daring swerve that left them all at sea. Then Fish goaled, and it was only a little uncertainty by Isherwood towards the olose that prevented a couple more tries being regis- tered. It was indeed a glorious victory, and unbiased Yorkshiremen were loud in their praise of the War- rington men. The majority of those present on Wed- nesday certainly look upon Warrington as the ultimate cup-winners.

Where all did so well, it is difficult to praise individuals. Still, the palm must be awarded to Hallam, who was certainly the best man on the field. He never made a solitary blunder and some of his saves were brilliant. G-uan on the other side is a fine player but he was eclipsed by the Warringtonian. Next comes Dai Davies who showed what a great half- back he is fast developing into. His work throughout was masterly and all Warrington's attacking move- ments came from his hand. Hockenhall seconded the Welshman's efforts in brilliant style, while the three- quarters were at their very best. The front rank deserves a hearty pat on the back too, for they fairly wore their big opponents down, Thomas and Board- man being particularly energetic. On the Bradford side, Gunn and Mosby were the only two men who did anything at all classy, the changes made in the back division appearing to play havoc. Haseltine was a complete failure, and the forwards belied their reputation as a great pack, after the first quarter of an hour.

The contrast between Saturday's and Wednesday's matches was this. At Broughton, Bradford were at their best and should have won, while Warrington were slightly below par. On Wednesday they ex- celled themselves and showed what great things they were capable of. On the two games, there is no ques- tion as to which is the cleverer side.

Warrington have been very busy cup-hunting this week. On Monday they took a full team to Leigh and made no mistake with the Mather-lane men. Fish scored the winning try with a superb single-handed effort, and Harris added another as the result of com- bination which foreshadowed Warrington's form. On the home side Burgess was here,there and everywhere and kicked a goal against his old club-mates but—l am glad he does not play for Warrington now! He was

,I as erratic as ever, m to have Ira- 7 and does not see , proved in the slightest. Generally speaking Leigh have a side which should have raised them above the , third place from the bottom of the League.

On Thursday evening Warrington left nothing to chance in their match at RuncJrn, for the result of this game decided the West Lancashire Competition and Warrington are the holders of this Cup for the third time in their history and twice in the last three seasons. The full cup team journeyed to Irwell-lane and trounced the sadly-degenerated Linnets. by 19 points to nil, Fish and Harris getting two tries each and Davies one, while Fish kicked, a couple of goals. By the way the latter has now aggregated 151 points during the present season, and at present is in his best form.

Since the last issue of the " Examiner " Warrington have drawn with Bradford, beaten Leigh, beaten Bradford in the replay, and beaten Runcorn with a total score of 36 points to 5. They are fairly on the move just now and it will take Salford all their tune to secure two points at Wilderspool to-day. In fact if the home side is not too tired, the Reds will get beaten. This match has an important bearing on the Salford and Bradford tie at the top. Bradford, too, will be extended to the utmost at Hunslet, and the ultimate champions are still unknown. To-day's game will, perhaps, decide the question.

For to-day's match with Salford, 'Warrington have selected the full team with the emception of Hallani for whom Knight plays.

The matches with Wigan and Huddersfield away next week will be played on Tuesday and Wednesday.

No fewer than nine excursions have been arranged by the two railway companies to convey .the War- rington supporters next Saturday. Four will depart from Bank Quay at intervals from 1-30 to 2-15, and the passengers will alight at Weaste station which is three minutes walk from the Salford ground. The Cheshire Lines committee have decided to run five specials to Manchester Central from 12-45 to 2-10, and there will be a service of cars from Daansgate to the ground.

Cricket. If the old saying that "well began is half done applies in the sporting sense, Warrington should have a successful season in store. • The result of Saturday's match was decidedly gratifying, Sefton being defeated by a majority of 35. While footballers hold possession of the field, enthusiasts cannot be expected to transfer their interest to the summer sport, and there were but 100 spectators at Arpley., Mr. E. A. Connor captained the Warrington side and, winning the toss, decided to have first turn at the wicket. The state of the ground after the heavy rain was more favourable to the bowlers than to the batsmen, and the result was that there were no heavy scores. The Warrington innings produced 71 runs, and the S3fton men were all dismissed for 36.

•

The feature of the match was the splendid bowling of Lynes, the home professional, who carried all before • him. With one exception he captured a wicket for every over, and his average worked out at 7 wickets for, 5 runs. He is to bo congratulated upon a brilliant performance.

IN AND AROUND WARRINGTON. Our Political Clubs.

[BY THE FREE CRITIC.]

A few remarks on our local political clubs at the present critical and serious time, when the affairs of the nation are being sadly mismanaged and a check placed on true progress, may perhaps not :be out of place. In the opinion of many, including even their best friends and habitues of the clubs, the clubs are not carrying out the mission for which they were designed:

NV-hat is their mission? Well in the first place to be the local central organising body of their own particular political creed ; also for the propagating of this creed among the electorate of the town. But in the main for the purpose of educating young men, so that they might in tie future be fitted to take their part in the political arena, and thus make the town in which they dwell a healthier and brighter place that what it is. In short the clubs were designed to be a means for the uplifting of the masses of the people. That is the true function of politics. 'Both Conservatives and Liberals who under- stand why they are Conservatives or Liberals acknow- ledge this, the only disagreement is as to the methods —the vital difference between the two parties being that the Liberals are generally in the van of progress, with the Conservatfves a long way behind. Well now, what as a matter of fact is our experi- ence of these clubs to-day? It appears to me that they are in the main social clubs ; places where young men and elderly men go to while away their spare time not as a rule to talk politics or even to read the nervspapers of the day—for the 'reading rooms are the least attended of all the rooms—but take a hand at solo ,whist, or nap, or for a game of billiards or shell out dominoes, &c., the stakes as a rule being on the small side. The games are occasionally inter- spersed with a casual call at or to the bar for a glass of bitter.

I have not a word to say against these social habits, except to say that they are being somewhat overdone. I am only describing what does take place. What I wish to suggest is, that the clubs should get back to what they,were designed for. In the winter season for one thing they should inaugurate monthly debates amongst the members upon the political and local questions of the day. The result would be that they would not only find out who were their ablest and best men but this would also be the means of educating the members, so that they would be able to take a more intelligent and practical interest in the affairs of the town and nation. It would also be the means of filling our local Parliament with abler men than have been sent for many :years. And it might also be the meransjof teaching men the value of a vote— and goodness only knows that intelligent voters are sadly needed at this juncture—so that another Govern meat may arise that shall bring Peace, Prosperity and Happiness into this land of ours. The present Government has lamentably failed. If the club managers would only realise that they have a responsibility in this direction it would be a good'thing for the town and, I believe, a stimulant to the growth and development of the clubs themselves.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

DtZUNK AT EIGLIT7-EXTRAORDINARY WARRINGTON CASE.•

The National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children brought an extraordinary case before the notice of the Warrington magistrates on Monday. William Birch, of Hulme Lock, Winwick, was charged with causing unnecessary suffering to his eight-year old nephew on April 2nd.—Mr. J. W. Knowles prosecuted and stated that on the date named, the wife of P.C. Woof!, of Hulme. was looking through the back window of the house and saw the defendant's nephew lying kicking on the ground. The boy was carried into the house and Mrs. Wooff noticed that he smelled of beer. When P.C. Woof! came in,the boy was unconscious. The officer gave him salt and water, which caused him to vomit, and in an hour or two he came round. There was no doubt that the child suffered seriously. Defendant admitted that he had allowed him to drink ale and "pop" supplied to him (defendant) at public houses which they had visited daring the afternoon. It was necessary that publicans should be protected from men of defendant's class who took small children into public houses. If the licencees had seen the child drink he beer, they would probably have been seriously dealt with.—Defendant, who pleaded guilty, 'stated that the boy accompanied him to several public- houses. He asked to be allowed to taste of the beer and 'pop" which witness ordered, and witness let him take several sips. He thought that in addition the lad must have had a drink from someone else's glass.—The Mayor said the Bench did not think defendant purposely caused the child suffering, and they would impose a line of 20s.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
SIX MONTHS FOR A SHOP LIFTER.

Six months imprisonment was the sentence passed upor Mary Jane McMiles, married woman, of Cross- street, at the Warrington Police Court on Monday. There were three charges against her viz :—Stealing dress skirt of the value of 7s. from the shop of Harry Buckley, pawnbroker, Orford-lane; a pair of boots, valued 235., from Messrs. Stead and Simpson's establish- ment in Horsemarket-street ; and a shawl, value 7s. 6d., from the shop a Messrs. G. H. and H. M. Jackson, pawnbrokers, Orford-lane. She pleaded guilty- -The Chief Constable said that if the police had thought fit they could have charged the prisoner with twice that number of robberies. She was obviously a confirmed shop lifter. She came to Warrington from Preston nine or ten months ago, and her husband, who was a respectable workingman, earned good wages and gave them to her. When the skirt was missed from Mr. Buckley's shop,the police were communicated with, and Superintendent Monaghan and Detective Meacock -searched prisoner's house. They found evidence of a quantity of clothing having been burnt in the fireplace. Prisoner said she had burnt a stolen skirt .and a number of other articles in addition to pawntickets relating to robberies. The boots and shawl mentioned in the present cases had been pledged. Prisoner had been making a practice of thieving ever -since she came to Warrington, and had had two months for shoplifting. She was an expert thief, the goods "being purloined from inside the shops.—Prisoner was •ent to gaol for six months.

Minor Cases.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

• 310NDA..v.=-Before the-Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, Councillor Thornton, Messrs. Birchall, Wright and Hayward. COMMERCIAL TRAVELLER FINE D. —H. Trafford, commercial traveller, 32, Fir-street, Southport, was summoned for travelling on the London and North- Western Railway from Crewe to Warrington on February -25th, without• paying the fare.—Mr. Fenna prosecuted and said the defendant was first seen at Bank Quay station in the 10-20 a.m. train for Crewe. On being asked for his ticket defendant said he had not booked to Crewe, and produced the out- ward half of a return ticket from Southport (St. Luke's) to Warrington. He paid the excess fare to Crewe and went on to that town. The same collector saw him when he returned to Warrington at 1.51 and asked him for his ticket. D4endant produced the return half of the Southport ticket and the collector noted it. He then went on to Wigan by the same train as the defendant, and saw the defendant again produce the SOuthport ticket. Defendant, therefore, had not paid the fare from Crewe to Warrington. On being spoken to by the collector, he said he was in a hurry at Crewe and offered to pay the fare. The collector refused to take it.—Defendant stated that in the hurry at Crewe, he omitted to book. He was troubled at the illness of his wife, and absent-mindedly presented the Southport ticket.—A fine of 30s. was imposed. SCENE ON THE RAILWAY—A SIGNALMAN THREATENED. —Robert Chadwick, 160, Liverpool-road, Isaac Hatch, 13, Elizabeth-street and Frank Hughes, Taylor-street, Lower Walton, were charged with trespassing on the London and North-Western Railway on Saturday, and with doing wilful damage to three signal lamps to the extent of 16s. Hughes was further charged with inter- fering with William Henry Robertson, a signalman on duty.—Mr. Fenna, who prosecuted, expressed regret that Hughes was in that position, as he was a servant of the company—The evidence showed that the defendants, who had had drink but were not drunk, trespassed on the railway line near Bank Quay Station and went to the signal box where Robertson was on duty. Hughes ascended the steps, entered the box, and made use of filthy and threatening language to Robertson, stating that he would throw him out. The other defendants remained at the bottom of the steps, and when Hughes rejoined them, they all commenced to kick at three signal lamps, smashing the glasses. Three railway detectives came upon the scene and took the men to the Police Station. On the way, Chadwick,who had a large pocket knife in his possession, threatened to • "rip one of the detectives from top to toe."—lt was admitted that Hughes had had more drink than his companions.—Mr. Steel, who appeared for Hughes, stated that the man had been in the employ of the company for four years and had borne a good character.

WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

Extensions at Winwick Street Branch•

Mr. T. Birc.hall presided at the quarterly meeting of the Warrington Equitable Industrial Co-operative Society Limited, which was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Monday evening. There was a good attendance, the president being supported by Mr. W. Jeffrey and Mr. A. H. Rathbone (managing secre- tary.)

AZIOTHER SUCCESSFUL QUARTER. WZNWICK STREET BRANCH TO BE EXTENDED.

The balance sheet showed that the sales for the quarter amounted to £36,482 3s. 4d., being an increase of £265 Bs. over last quarter, and £4,697 9s. 10d. over the Corresponding quarter of last year. The CHAIRMAN pointed out that the sales last quarter constituted a record, but the society had ex- ceeded them during the past quarter by over £2OO. When they considered the bad state of trade,they might congratulate themselves upon having done exceedingly well. Referring to the infusion of new blood into the committee, the chairman said they had about as fine a body of practical businessmen to manage the affairs of the society as they could possibly find. The outlook was very favourable. They had taken steps to build two new shops in Winwick-street, as the present accom- modation was not sufficient to cope with the business. It was proposed to separate the grocery and provision departments. The originel plans provided for the ex- tension so that it was just a matter of completing them. Mr. Birchall proceeded to allude to the powerful influence of the co-operative movement, and to its enormous extent. In regard to co-operative pro- duction, he lnoked forward to that branch of their work with great hope, as he beliet ed that it was destined to solve the problem of the troubles between capital and labour. If workingmen became their own 6mployer9, they woukl take more interest in their work. The balance sheet was adopted. COMMITTEE BUSINESS.

Mr. RATHBONE reported that at a meeting of the committee it was decided, in view of the expression of opinion by the general meeting, to grant £lO each to Messrs. Robert Cook and William Jones (retiring mem- bers) as an acknowledgment of their past services. Sympathetic reference was made to the death of Mr. Joseph Albinson, and it was decided to forward a letter of condolence to the family.

The plans for the extension of the Winwick street branch were oonsidered and the manager was instructed to arrange the details with Mr. William Owen, the architect.

The question of the proposed remuneration of the committee was discussed, and is was considered ad- visable to defer the matter until further information had been obtained with referenoe to the course adopted by other societies. . .

The CHAIRMAN remarked that the success of the Stockton Heath branch had far exceeded their antici- pations, the sales during the quarter having averaged £l7O per week. Mr. CaowTHER. inquired when tha Bewsey-road branch would be opened, as the premises were now unoccupied. The SECRETARY said it was considered advisable to leave the matter over for a few weeks. The premises had been empty about a fortnight. The minutes were approved. THE REMUNERATION OF THE COMMITTEE.

This question was raised by Mr. W. Haigh who moved that the society bhould pay the committee on the high- est scale which it was ascertained was paid in other towns. He was not in favour of a cheese-paring policy, and if they agreed upon a fixed remuneration they would know exactly what the committee were receiving, and the amount would appear on the balance sheet. The CHAIRMAN suggested that the society should adopt the medium scale—that was £2 per quarter,which was paid by the Runcorn Society. The highest amount was that granted by the Manchester Society, which, of course was a very large concern. Mr:CROWTHER moved that 2s. per meeting be paid. The CHAIRMAN: That is the lowest. It was pointed out that if the Runcorn scale were adopted the cost would be £1.04 per annum. Mr. WINSKILL was in favour of deferring the matter. The new members of the committee had not yet had an opportunity of proving their worth. Mr. W. JEFFREY moved that the society adhere to the present system. Mr. FARRAR seconded. Mr. N. Cool( moved that the committeo be paid £5 per annum. Mr. G. WALLINGTON taconded, and said he was afraid thatif they made the remuneration high there would be a great deal of competition for seats on the committee. On a vote being taken it was decided to pay the com- mittee £5 each per annum.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW COMMITTEE-MAN.

The meeting then proceeded to elect a member of the committee in place of the late Mr. Joseph Albinson. The gentlemen proposed were Messrs. Ch arles Hibbert, Nathan Cook, W. Haigh, and Joseph Owen. On a show of hands being taken, Mr. Hibbert was elected by a good majority. THE SECRETARY'S SERVICES.

Mr. WiNsicia-r-eTuinrded the members that this year, Mr. Rathbone would complete forty years' service with the society. He suggested that a great meeting should be held, to be addressed by prominent co-operators, and at the same time opportunity could be taken of congratulating the secretary upon his valuable work.

It was pointed out that workingmen were already being appealed to at the various works, and it was not considered necessary for the society to subscribe.

PUBLIC_ BENEFIT. UNDER the CLOCK for BEST

BOCTS in LANCASHIRE.—Advt. The Fire Brigade's Work in Country Districts.

At the meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Coun- cil held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, the clerk (Mr. G-. F. Ashton) reported that the Town Clerk of Warrington had written enclosing copy of the follow- ing resolution of the Town Council That local neighbouring authorities be informed that the fire engines will not be permitted to leave the borough, unless such authorities are prepared to contribute to the establishment expenses of this department."—The resolution, it was stated, would effect Stockton Heath, Walton, Grappenhall, and adjoining parishes.—lt was decided to refer the matter to the Parish Councils and Parochial Committees of the townships concerned. —The Town Council's resolution was also under con- sideration at the meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council on Wednesday.—The clerk was in- structed to write to the Town Clerk asking what subscription the Corporation required. The matter will be further discussed at the next meeting.

UNDER the CLOCK for GOOD BOOTS.—Advt The Late Mr. R. Garnett. The many friends of the Garnett family will be interested in the publication of "sketches and letters by Robert and Mary Garnett," which Mrs. Garnett, Hall Nook, Penketh, has recently issued with the aid of Messrs Mackie and Company Limited. In a prefa- tory note, Mrs. G-arnett says " These stray letters and papers are printed in order to enshrine the drawings of my husband, Robert Garnett, and are lovingly dedicated to his memory." In Wesleyan circles particularly the book will be welcomed, for the letters descriptive of Egypt and Pales tine are brightly written and their perusal will recall many a lecture at Penketh and elsewhere. The late Mr. Garnett was not a great black and white artist, but he turned his knowledge of draughtsmanship to pleasant and practi- cal use when on his travels, and the numerous draw- ings reproduced are interesting. The more preten- tious are certainly those illustrative of travels in Northern France and Prussia, and here Mr. Garnett's obvious love of the beautiful in architecture has stimulated him to good work. We might add that the book is neatly bound in cl oth and well printed on art paper. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING GP TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Funeral of Mr. Thomas Hatton. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. The Present Position of Sunday Schools.

There was a large attendance at the quarterly meet- ing_ of the Warrington Sunday School Teachers' Asso- ciation held in St. Paul's schools, Kendrick-street, on Monday evening under the presidency of the Rev. W. Bracecamp. An interesting address on "Sunday schools—their present position" was delivered by Mr. Herbert J. Westbrook. Churchmen, he said, must ever be strong supporters of Sunday schools—and especially Northern Churchmen, for the North of England had long been noted for its support of Sun- day schools, and especially for the feature of the large attendance of adult scholars of both sexes. Mr. West- brook proceeded to refer to the position before the passing of the Education Act of 1870 and after, and to the opinions then expressed to the need for the overhauling of the Sunday school system and its establishment on a more systematic basis. The tendency of day schools to become more and more secular was felt then, and it was recognised that -Sunday school work had and would become more increasingly important as time went on. He con- sidered that they stood pretty much in a similar position to-day as in 1869 and 1872. The increasing nuntber of subjects now taught in the day schools tended to thrust aside or squeeze into the narrowest limits the teaphing of religious knowledge. The spirit of absolute indifference and agnosticism and the aggressive and open attacks on our Christian faith demanded that every effort for the building up and de- fence of our common Christianity should be made. To him it appeared unquestionable that the need and work of Sunday schools was all, important. The Sunday school must be in form and in fact the nursery and training ground of the Church and they were all, both clergy and laity alike, called upon to make greater efforts for the strengthening and sus- taining of Sunday school work. Mr. Westbrook advanced a number of suggestions to increase the use- fulness of Sunday schools, including the holding of teachers' classes and a more general revival of the old system of the catechising of scholars by the clergy. —An interesting discussion followed, and Mr. West- brook was cordially thanked.

ITIRRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Election of Chairman anti Committees.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians— the first of the newly-constituted Board—was held on Wednesday. At the outset, Mr. J. C. Sutton, the clerk, occupied the chair, and there were present :—Mrs. Clay, Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Wilkinson, Rev. r. R. Matthews, Rev. Father Mercer, Dr. Patterson. Messrs. T. H. Ashcroft, J. W. Knowles, A. Brooks, J. Backhouse. Bolton, J. Clarke, S. Davies. R. Forshaw, W. Hickman W. Houghton, A. J. Jolley, G. Mather, Flanagan E. L. Bent, J. Payton, G. Thompson, G. rurton, J. Ball T. Ritchie, P. Longton and S. Sutton (deputy clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), A. Wright (superintendent, Padgate Schools).

===== 0002601_19040423_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED CO-OPTATION OF MEMBERS.

A preliminary private conference of Guardians wai held for the purpose of considering the advisability of co-opting guardians who had done good service on the Board. A proposal was brought forward to co-opt two members, but the proposition was defeated.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
THI4 CRAIRMANSHIP.

The CLERK, in introducing the business of the Board proper, said the chair was made for him in the 1860's. (Applause.) He was at that time the youngest in years of the members of the Board. For some years he had been the senior of the seniors of the members of the Board, and he felt grateful that he had been permitted to be present at so many annual meetings and other meetings of the Board. He felt it was of considerable advantage in having been blessed so bug with health, which permitted him to be there so long. (Hear,hear.) Mr. DAVIES congratulated the Clerk upon the position he then occupied. He (Mr. Davies) had remembered the Clerk having it for the past 15 years, and during that time there had been many changes. He had pleasure in proposing that Mr. Brooks be re-elected chairman of the Board for the ensuing year. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Brooks had proved himself equal to the duties of the office and he had in fact exceeded their expectations of him. (Hear, hear.) He was well aware that there were members present who were of opinion that life was very short and that no gentleman ought to occupy the chair fcr more than one year. (Mr. HicKAIAN: "Hear, hear.)" Continuing, Mr. Davies said he thought he had spoken the sentiments of those gentlemen—(laughter)—but he did not agree with them altogether, because there were many members who might not feel equal to the duties of that position, and therefore he thought it ought to be held for two years but not for more. It was especially the case now, when there were so many new forces—new brooms who probably wanted to sweep clean—(laughter)—that they should have a chairman who was firm and impartial. He thought Mr. Brooks was the right man, as he was firm genial and strictly impartial. (Applause). It was therefore with the greatest pleasure and confldenae that he proposed the motion. _ _ _

Mr. KNowLEs seconded and remarkei that Mr. Brooks was really a country squire in his own district— (laughter)—and he was able to devote his whole time to public affairs. He was a good business man and had travelled a good deal and had bad a great deal of experience. Mr. W. HouGirroN, Mr. FORSHAW and Mrs. CLAY supported. Mr. HECKNIA.N said he was going to submit another name. as he believed in honours being divided. One year was quite sufficient for any man to hold the posi- tion of chairman, unless there were exceptional circum- stances. No one had greater regard for Mr. Brooks than he had, but others ought to have the chance of holding his position. He proposed that Mr. George Mather be the chairman for the ensuing year. Mr. ASEICEtOFT seconded, and pointed out that Mr. Mather had beea a very hard-working member of the Board.

Mr. MA.TRER observed that lie would not like to have the position at the present time. Mr. Brooks, he thought, ought to hold the office for anotber year. The name of Mr. Mather having been withdrawn, Mr. Brooks was unanimously elected chairman. Mr. BuooKs, on taking the chair, thanked the Guar- dians sincerely for the kind way in which they had sup- ported him, and hoped they would do good work during the coming year.

SITES FOR OFFICES COMMITTEE.

MR. JOLLEY AND THE BOARD'S RESPONSIBILITY.

Mr. THOMPSON moved that this committee be abol- ished. It was quite unnecessary. • Mr. FORSHAW Seconded.

Mr. JOLLEY hoped that the motion would not be carried, as there were responsibilities of which new members and perhaps some old members of the Board might not be cognisant. They were bound to provide offices for the accommodation of the superintendent registrar, and the Rural District Council were also entitled to offices without charge. The arrangements with regard to the registrar's office, were generally un- satisfactory and the registrar general bad threatened them with pains and penalties on account of it. The mere existence of the committee was a sort of safety valve, and it must not be thought that by dropping the committee they could get rid of their responsibility. Mr. THOMPSON pointed out that the expenses of the Borough and the County were so very high that he did not think it was a very wise thing to continue the Sites Committee. The question of offices was more a ques- tion for the Rural Council. The guardians had a Boatd Room and it was the Rural Council that wanted offices.

Mr. KNOWLES said the Rural Council had a very good meeting room at present in Bewsey Chambers. Tne CIIA.IRMANT observed that the Rural District Council Made a recommendation some time ago that new offices should be built.

Mr. HICKMAN thought thesites for °aces Committee ought to be re-named "the Watching Committee." (Laughter.) The committee was watching for a suit- able site and he thought the members ought to be re-elected.

It was decided to re-appoint the committee, and the following were put upon it: Dr. Patterson, Messrs. Houghton, A. J. Jolley and W. Hickman.

THE REPRESENTATIVE ON THE POOR LAW TJNION.

The work of the asso3iation of Poor Law Unions was of a constructive character and not like a conference where the members went to learn something. The proposition was carried. He was endeavouring to arrange with some of the assistant over- seers to pay their calls.

THE FEES FOR VACCINATION.

The CLERK said in his early ex erience of the Board, the bill for vaccination by Dr. Gornall was £25 per quarter, and the vaccination returns were as favourable as they were to-day. That morning he had to ask for the passing of the following cheques for vaccination fees :—Dr. Pes,cocke, £96 Is. ' • Dr. Bennett, £Bl 18s. 6d.; Dr. Valentine, £73 9s. Dr. Dowling and Dr. Sephten's bills had not been sent in. Mr. HICKMAN pointed out that the population was much larger now. The CLERK stated that during ths recent smallpox scarelt was considered desirable to re-vaccinate some children under ten years of age. He furnished the Local Government Board with a list of the children re- vaccinated and asked for their sanction to the payment of the fees. The Board had replied, stating that under the circumstances, they sanctioned the payment by the Guardians of fees to Dr. Bennett for re-vaccinating 48 cases and Dr. Peacock° for re-vaccinating 43 cases.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Mr. A R Norman Elected Chairman:

The annual meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. There were present, in addition to the Rural District Councillors, the Rev. Father Donlevy, Messrs. A. R. Norman, T. Lea, J. W. Temperley, W. L. Morris, J. Stokes, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (deputy clerk), T. Cook and W. R. DePrez (relieving officers.)

===== 0002601_19040423_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN.

Mr. Stokes, the retiring chairman, proposed that Mr. A. R. Norman be elected chairman for the ensuing year. Mr. Norman, he said, had ably discharged the duties of vice-chairman, and he (Mr. Stokes) could assure the Board that he would fill the position of chairman with credit to himself and to the Board. Mr. C. E. Ilainaker seconded and the motion was heartily carried. In returning thanks Mr. Norman said he was fully conscious that in electing him to that position, the Board was placing him in the succession of a long line of honourable gentlemen who had occupied the position in the past. His chief desire would be to keep untarnished the chair of the Runcorn Board of Guardians and to do all he could to maintain its honourable traditions. He believed the work the Board had to do was one of the most important trusts any public men could have—that was to look after the wel- fare of the needy poor. So far as his experience went —and he had bean connected with the authority for a considerable number of years—he believed that Board was second to none in trying to do its duty to the poor, and at the same time to consider the interests of the ratepayers. WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Mr T. Halin Re-elected Chairman.

UNUSUAL PR9CEDURF..

The annual meeting of the Warrington Rural Distiict Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon. There were present :—Dr. Murray, Messrs. Brooks, Ball, Knowles, Mather, Forshaw, Backhouse, Longton,Thompson,Houghtnn, with Dr. Saphton (medi- cal officer of health), Messrs. J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), F. Kerfoot (inspector of nuisances), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), and H.H.Burn- age (assistant surveyor).

===== 0002601_19040423_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CLERK'S LONG PUBLIC SERVICES.

The CLERK was elected to take the chair, pending the election of Chairman for the ensuing year. He remarked that when appointed that morning at the annual meeting of the Board of Guardians temporarily to occupy the chair, he referred to his appointment as permanent chairman of that Board in the eighteen sixties. He might, upon the Couhcirs appointment of him temporarily to occupy their chair, say that his official experience of the work of the Rural District and Parochial authorities dated back to the eighteen forties. Wherever the records of proceedings in those years of the Courts Leet of the then Right Honourable Lord Lilford for the manors of the two Sankeys and of Burtonwood, of the Rev. James J. Hornby for the large manor of Winwick, and of Lord Winmar- leigh and Sir Charles Tempest for the manor of Rixton might be, they were in his handwriting, he being in attendance with the steward of the manor. Through- out the various subsequent modes of local Rural and Sanitary administration, he had had more or less of official association with the work. (Hear, hear.)

ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN. AN UNUSUAL COURSE. MR. HALTON APPOINTED.

Mr. HOUGHTON said that as the Council was a small body numerically it could be aug- mented with very great advantage by the co-op- tion of a suitable person as chairman. Mr. Halton had served his parish well and had performed very great services for the Rural Council. As he (Mr. Houghton) had said before, he did not wish to speak a syllable in disparagement of Mr. Thompson who had supplanted Mr. Halton and he wanted to leave aside all local personal feeling. Having regard to the valuable services ren- dered by Mr. Halton, and the really hard work he had done, he (Mr. Houghton) begged to move that Mr. Halton be co-opted as chairman of the Council. Mr. THOMPSON: You cannot co-opt anyone on the Council. You can elect a chairman from outside, but you cann,,t co-opt anyone. Mr. Houcauros: lam obliged to Mr. Thompson for the technical point. We can proceed direct with the election of chairman, and I move that Mr. Halton be elected to the chair for the ensning twelve months. Mr. FORSHA.W seconded. He had sorved under Mr. Halton for the past twelve months, and thought he had proved himself a very able chairman. They could not do better than let him retain the chair.

_ Mr. BALL had great pleasure in supporting. He had served with Mr. Halton for a great number of years on the old highway Board and also on the Rural Council ever since the latter authority came into , operation. He had the pleasure of proposing Mr. Halton as chair- man last year, and he had never had occasion to regret his action. Mr. KNOWLES also supported.

THOMPSON moved an amendment that Mr. Brooks be elected chairman. He did not believe in bringing anyone in from outside, unless it was under exceptional circumstances such as electing Mr. parr as Mayor of Warrington. In his opinion, the mean- ing of the Act was that if they had no person eligible in the Council, they had the option of electing an outsider. In that Council they were very fortunate in having gentlemen capable and able to take the chair. He might mention the names of Mr. Brooks, Mr. Knowles, Mr. Mather and Dr. Murray, who were all capable of performing the duties. He did not think the Act ever intended that defeated candidates should be brought in in the way suggested in the present case. He wanted to know what Mr. Halton's position would be in that Council. He (Mr. Thompson) had been returned to represent Great Sankey but he found that one gentleman stated at the last Council meeting that there ought to be someone on the Council to represent the agricultural interests of the parish, directly or indirectly. Therefore the speaker ! expressed the hope that Mr. Halton's retirement would be only temporary. Mr. Mather, continued Mr. Thomp- son stated at the same meeting that he felt the loss acutely and hoped it would not ba a long time before 'Great Sankey regained itself. (Laughter.) Mr. Hough- ton also seemed to forget that he (Mr. Thompson)had been a member of the Council before. He was not speaking personally now, but he wanted to know what position Mr. Halton would hold in the Council. Mr. Krtowf,Es : He would not represent Sankey. The CLERK said Mr. Halton would be the chairman of the Council with the usual pi;ivileges attaching to the office. He would, of course, be entitled to vote. Mr. THOMPSON : Would he have a casting vote? The DEPUTY CLERK: No.

Mr. THOMPSON, coatinuing, said Mr. Houghton had referred to the scarcity of members of the Council. It would be a very graceful thing if they gave Burtonwood another r epresentative. They could get that permission by applying to the County Council. Mr. KNOWLES : Would'nt that be an insult to the present able member? Mr. BACKHOUSE : Give Sankey another representa- tive.

Mr. FORSHA.W : I think we had better keep to the business in hand. Mr. THOMPSON : This question was brought forward when I was on the Council before. Buttonwood is en- titled to another representative, and I hope it will get One. It has agricultural land to the extent of 44,192 acres- Mr. FORSHAW : You represent Sankey. Mr. THOMPSON said that at Penketh the District Coun- cillor was a doctor, and at Middleton he was a lawyer. He did not think Great Sankey would take much hum in regard to its representation. He hoped the Council would elect Mr. Brooks to the chairmanship. Mr. LONGTON seconded. He had a very great objec- tion to bringing an outsider in like that. It was a pro- cedure that some time they might be sorry, for. Mr. BROOKS said he felt highly flattered at the remarks made by the mover of the amend- ment. He hardly thought that the chairmanship was a position that he could take under the present circumstances. He had been appointed chair- man of the Board of Guardians, and it would be unique if he occupied both offices. He did not think that was at all desirable. The interests of the two authorities did occasionally clash somewhat, and he thought the chairmanships should be divided. He supported Mr. Halton's election. Mr. MATHER agreed with Mr. Thompson and Mr. Longton that it would be a very bad precedent to set. He had no personal objection to Mr. Halton occupying the chair, but he thought that Council was strong and able enough without going outside for a representative to assist them. Mr. HOUGHTON bontended that the object of the Government in giving them the privilege of electing a gentleman from outside, was to meet such a position as they .iiow found themselves in. Mr. MATHER: To enable us to elect a defeated candidate? _ _

Mr. HOUGHTON: Look at the number of Parish Councils which have never had anyone but a co-opted chairman, because the members are so few that they can scarcely get a quorum sometimes. This Council is very small numerically. Mr. LONGTQN: But we always get a.guorum.

Mr. HOUGHTON: We have irot-much-over a quorum very often. I feel very strongly on the matter. The CLERK said that as Mr. Brooks declined to stand, the members must give either an affirmative or negative vote in the matter. A vote was then taken, five voting for Mr. Haltonand four against. Mr. Halton's name was again put to the meeting, and the election was carried without opposition.

--111;71INO'vri.us : I propose thaeive telephone to Mr. Halton and ask him to take the ?hair.

Mr. THOMPSON : I propose that we do not. The matter was put to the meeting, and four voted in favour of telephoning to Mr. Halton. • The CLERK: That is not a majority. • THE VICE-CHAIRMANSHIP. Mr. HOUGHTON proposed that Dr. Murray be elected vice-chairman. Hitherto Dr. Murray had not been put forward for any of the places of honour, although he i had taken great interest n the work of that Council and the Board of Guardians. Mr. THOMPSON seconded. Mr. KNOWLES said that if Mr. Brooks had accepted the chairmanship, he would have seconded the present proposition with pleasure. They must not overlook the fact that Mr. Brooks served as vice-chairman last year, and that as vice-chairman he was on the stepping stone to the chairmanship. When he became chairman it would be an opportune occasion to propose Dr. Murray as vice-chairman. Otherwise it looked to him uncommonly like kicking Mr. Brooks downstairs. (Laughter.) He did not agree with such a procedure, and proposed that Mr. Brooks be elected vice-chairman. Mr• HOUGHTON said he had unintentionally over- looked Mr. Brooks, and, with Mr. Thompson's per- mission, he would withdraw his motion. Mr. THOMPSON agreed. Dr. MURRAY seconded Mr. Knowles's proposition. The CLERK remarked that the position seemed to be the same as in the case of the lovers' tiff—"lt was all very well to dissemble your love, but why did you kick met downstairsl !" (Laughter). Mr. BROOKS was then unanimously elected vice- chairman, and took the chair for the afternoon. ' -

ALTERATION IN THE DATES. OF MEETINGS.

It wa:;-.:l;i7a;tl- that the meetings of the Council should be held on the first Wednesday in each month at 2 p.m. •

PROPOSED ROAD IMPROVEMENT. The CLERK reported the receipt of a letter from the Town Clerk stating that the Corporation proposed to sell the land adjoining Longford depot off the Winwick road. It was suggested that opportunity might be

taken, before the Corporation disposed of the land, or widening and improving the road. The SURVEYOR pointed out that when the South* Lancashire Tramways Company extended their system to Longford, they would be bound by arrangement to makea 27ft. carriageway and to provide a footpath of the existing width. That, of course, would mean a con- siderable widening of the road.

The Council di'd not deem it- necessary to take any action in the matter.

THE BROOK AT LONGFORD-A COMPLAINT.

The Rev. Cation Penrhyn wrote OTii-I;eTtlii-Of the Winwick Parish Council, urging the Council to take steps to have the brook at Longford Bridge cleaned out. Owing to the water being held up, the land above was injured, and a serious nuisance, which was likely to be injurious to public health, was caused. The INSPECTOR reported that the water was held up because all kinds of rubbish were thrown into it by children. Instructions would be given for the brook te be cleaned out. RIXTON MORTUARY QUESTION.

The Clerk to the Rixton Parish Council wrote in- quiring if the building situated at the north end of the village cemetery could, in case of necessity, be used as a mortuary. The CLERK was instructed to intimate that the Coun- cil had no objection to the use of the building as a. mortuary. LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD AND FEARNHEAD SEWAGE. MORE DISCUSSION ABOUT SANKEY BROOK. The Looil Government Board wrote stating that they had had under consideration the annual report of the Medical Officer of Health of the Council. Theynoticed that in two reports Dr. Sephton had referred to the need for a sewerage system at Fearnhead, and the Board now inquired what steps the District Council had taken or proposed to take in the matter.

Mr. MATHER said he was afraid that if they embarked upon a sewerage scheme, they would run the parish into the bankruptcy court. A special rate would be required, and the township could not stand one. The CLERK remarked that he thought it was a great mistake for anyone in the district to have objected to what the Council contemplated doing in connection with the discharge of sewage from Winwick Asylum. If that had not been interfered with, they would inevit- ably to-day have ha-I the whole of Sankey Brook cleared out, and they would have had a proper place for the reception of the sewage of the whole district. That must come about sooner or later, as far as the brook was concerned. He remembered that when part of Poulton- with-Fearnhead was added to the borough, the Town Clerk stated in the course of correspondence that the Corporation would undertake to provide for the discharge of Fearnhead sewage. Mr. KNOWLES observed that with regard to Winwick Asylum the proposll was to make a larger sewer, to turn the asylum sewage into it, and empty it into Sankey Brook. Surely there was enough refuse in the brook without putting more into it. If they couldnot get 3 remedy now when it was in such a bad state, how were they going to obtain a remedy when its condition was made worse?

The CLERIC : There is a remedy. It is a question of carrying it out. Mr. KNOWLES : Why do we allow that thick, stink- ing mud to be turned into the brook all along the waY from here to Bold, if we can find a remedy? Why has it gone on for years and years? The CLERK said that with regard to the accumulation of filth, it was only a question of degree. If they had added to the refuse already turned into the brook, the Fearnhead and Winwick sewage, they must inevitably have carried out the arrangement that they had frivol- ously put away for twenty years to come. It was decided to refer the Local Government Beard's letter to the Poultons Parish Council.

BUTTERMILK BRIDGE. The CLERK read a letter from the clerk to the Sanker Parish Council stating that the footpath leading across the fields to Buttermilk Bridge was in need of repairs and that a length of the bottom rail of )he bridge was missing. Mr. THOMPSON said the bridge was in a most serious condition, and an accident might happen there ear moment. In that case the Council woul,d be liable. 11° suggested that the surveyor should attend to the bridge.

The SURVEYOR said the work would be done at mace. They had a difficulty in keeping the bridge there at all- People removedpieces of the woodwork for firewood. If a stone bridge was erected, he really believed theY would remove some of it for " ashley." HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

Dr. SF.PHTON reported that during the month there had been 23 births and 39 deaths. The death rate NOS equal to 41.07, against 41, the ayerage for the past three months, and 30.4 for the corresponding period last yea,r• Twenty-five deaths ocourred in Winwick Asylum, and, exclusive of these, the death rate for the district was 14.7.

LONGFORD DEPOT. The INSPECTOR reported that during the past month considerable progress had been made in the alterations at the depot. A new boiler had been put down for motive power and would be working shortly. The con- densing tower was complete and the reservoir had bee). constructed. On the whole, things were fairlY satisfactory. Mr. KNOWLES : The smell from there has not been noticeable for the past week, but we have had an es° wind all the time. (Laughter.) The report was approved.

ST. PAUL'S DOME POINTS THE

WAY.

Some things in this world, even though the purpose in creating or constructing them may have been raorg or less selfish, appear to be ordained to do good in sow? way to the people at large. Thus, St. Paul's Cathedrto was built simply as a place of worship, yet it is a sort of hub to the great wheel of London; a monumeg whereby to identify and locate the city. For magi miles outside of London you can see the great dome St. Paul's swelling up in the air; and you have only te say to the inquiring stranger who is seeking the centr! of the largest city in the world," You see the dome g.lt the Cathedral? Yes. Well make straight for it, and'" will lead you into the heart of London." FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

The middle-class woman has of late years tmproved greatly in the selection of her gowns and hats, besides evincing a niceness hitherto foreign to her in regard to the details of the toilette, her boots and gloves being as neat and well-fitting as those of her wealthier sisters, even if of less costly material. It is not so long ago when her best dresses were more to be remarked because of the gorgeous- ness of the fabrics and the brilliancy of the colours thrown promiscuously together, than for their harmony with her surroundings and sphere in life, her ambition being to outshine all others by apeing a wealth she did not possess, and expressing her tastes in cheap and tawdry imitations of costly materials. Now, however, it is far otherwise, although one every now and then comes across the excep- tion which goes to prove the general rule.

A NEAT TOQUE.

This has, no doubt, been greatly brought about by the introduction of moderate-priced ready made garments, neatly • and simply made, and the _advance of refinement in the world of dress. The example set by the leaders of fashion in exploiting (a simplicity which is often as costly as it is simple) of costume for everyday wear has had its effect especially on the toilettes designed for street wear. The influence of the tailor-made gown pervades all outdoor garments, although the severe characteristic of this style of garment has beenrelaxed of late.

A neat blouse and a short, well-fitting skirt will form one of the most popular styles of morning dress this season if worn in con- junction with a neat toque, such as is given in my first sketch. The toque is of straw of a fanciful make, and is quite devoid of trimming save for a stiff rosette, also made of straw. It is completed by a chenille spotted and bor- dered veil worn loosely over the face in the newest style, and after being tied at the back the ends are allowed to form a sort of cache- peigne at the back. It is not always very easy to adjust a veil successfully in this manner so as to allow the ends to fall gracefully over the hair, and as these veils are bought with the ends finished by the border, I advise the iniending wearer neatly folds as much fulness

as is left after trying the veil on the hat with which it is to be worn, into flat pleats and tacks them securely into place so that there is no tying necessary. The veil will fall much better if this is done as without the assistance of a clever maid the result is otherwise apt to prove more unsightly than fashionable..

- The pretty bl-puse- depicted is for afternoon wear and is made of fine cream nuns veiling with a folded silk collar and front. Two deep revers trimmed with passementerie are arranged at either side and extend from the shoulder to the waist. The sleeves, which are full from the elbow downwards, are caught in at the wrists by a tight cuff. A pretty narrow tie of the latest style is knotted in front with little gold catches in the form of rings. There are many varieties of these narrow ties some- times expressed in coloured silks, which, after being passed round the neck, are • passed through an ornamental ring. Blouses are particulearly pretty this season, and unless ex- pressed in cream or white, are of the same tone of colour as the skirt worn with them. There is no longer that vivid contrast of colour which there used to be between the bodice and skirt, and a uniformity of dress is approved. There are some very pretty blouses for morning wear of white cashmere piped with colours and trimmed with buttons of the same colour as the pipings. More dressy are the blouses of cream silk with deep yokes of lace and embroidery which extend down the upper

A PICTURE HAT

part of the arms. Others, again, have the o'er edge of the yoke finished off by two or three shaped frills, which form a V at the back and front and fall over the waistband. The latter style is not suited for wear under a coat and is reserved for indoor wear.

Very charming indeed is the picture hat given in my last sketch. The hat is made of cream Spanish lace stretched over a frame and lined in front with tucked chiffon of the same colour. The crown is draped with lace and a long lace veil extends from the back and can be worn falling down the back or brought round to the front and pinned to the bodice. A beautiful rose with buds and leaves nestles under the brim and is either pink or pale yellow, as suits the complexion of the wearer. The shape is at once fashionable and becoming and the hat would be found very useful throughout the summer. A black hat ex- pressed in the same materials, with a deep crimson rose, would make a .very effective article of headgear. The veils which fall down the back and are not intended for use out merely as an adjunct of dress are, we are told, to be worn very long, and old lace veils will be pressed into service by their fortunate possessorsd
