GRAPPENHALL PARISH COUNCIL.

The newly-elected Parish Council for Grappenhall met in the National School, on Tuesday evening. There 'were present : Messrs. J. C. Parr,Avery,Wareing,Jenkins, Ellison, Rathbone, Smith, Spence, Warburton, John Bradford, with Mr. S. J. \Noodcock (clerk).

===== 0002601_19040423_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHAIRMANSHIP.

Mr. Warburton proposed a -vote of thanks to Mr. Parr for having occupied the chair during the past year. Mr. Smith seconded, and said he hoped Mr. Parr would be unanimously re-elected. Mr. Parr returned thanks for the Council's recogni- tion of his services. They had worked very harmoniously together and he hoped he had fulfilled the duties of chairman to their satisfaction. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Spence then proposed and Mr. Jenkins seconded that Mr. Parr be re-elected chairman. Mr. Jenkins thought the affairs of the Parish Council would be quite safe in the hands of Mr. Parr. The proposition was unanimously agreed to. Mr. Parr, in reply, said he thanked the Connell for the renewal of their confidenee. It had not been a difficult task, because he thought they had all worked harmoniously together, and for the good of the parish. In accepting their nomination and resuming the duties of chairman, he felt the same spirit would animate the Council during the coming year. (Hear, hear.) He was sorry to see one or two vacancies in their ranks,but he apprehended that they had an accession of very capable and influential gentlemen, who would uphold the interests of the parish.

NEW OVERSEERS. Messrs. Boardman and Warburton, who have filled the office of overseers, for many years, resigned from the office. Messrs. Avery and George Ashton were appointed. THE ASSISTANT OVERSEER'S SALARY INCREASED. The next business was the appointment of an assist- ant overseer. Mr. Woodcock said that at the last audit, the auditor recommended that the salary, at present £3B, should be increased so as to include the cost of travelling and incidental expenses. The auditor stated that the salary, considering the ratable value of the parish, was comparatively rather low and he thought an advance would not be out of place.--- In 1892 when he was appointed assistant overseer, there were 209 assessments and a ratable value of 2 7,500. There were now 307 assessments and the ratable value had increased by £2,000. This involved extra work. He had taken the trouble to ascertain what was paid in neighbouring parishes. Thelwall with a ratable value of £5,500 paid the assistant overseer £35, and Stockton Heath with a ratable value of £9,800 paid £4O. In Stockton Heath the rates were easier to collect owing to the owners being fewer in number. On the motion of Mr. Wareing, seconded by Mr. Bradford, Mr. Woodcock was re-elected assistant over- seer, and a discussion ensued on the proposed increase of his salary. Mr. Jenkins asked what was Mr. Woodcock's stipend when he received the appointment. Mr. Woodcock said that when he was appointed some twelve years ago he received £26. Mr. Smith : If be is not satisfied, let him send his resignation in. Mr. Wareing asked Mr. Woodcock what his expenses amounted to, and he was informed about £2 10s. per annum. It was proposed by Mr. Wareing that the salary should be increased from £3B to £4O per annum, inclusive of travelling and incidental expenses. Mr. Jenkins moved an amendment that the salary remain as at present. His reason for doing so was that Mr. Woodcock had received an advance of something like £l3 since his appointment. Mr. Woodcock: £ll 10s. Mr. Smith seconded. The amendment was lost,only tke proposer and seconder voting for it.

AN INACCURATE NOMINATION PAPER.

THE CHAIRMAN'S RULING IMPUGNED.

Mr. Jenkins moved the following tesolgtion :—"That we regret the instructions of the clerk to the chairman of the annual parish meeting, whereby a parochial elector was deprived of his right to nomination for the Parish Council, and trust that in the future he will exercise greater care in carrying out the provisions of the Local Government Board Act of 1894." Mr. Jenkins said his object in reverting to the subject was to try and prevent, if possible, a recurrence of the displeasure and disappointment which were witnessed at the annual parish meeting held last month when a nomination paper was handed in on behalf of Mr. George Hankey, coal dealer,Knutsford-road. A slight discrepancy was made, the Christian name and the surname having been placed in the wrong column. It was a trivial mistake, but nevertheless the paper was declared invalid. Mr. Jenkins quoted from the Local Government Board Act, which, he stated, laid it down that when a mistake had been made in the Win,'b up of a nomination paper, in regard to the name and plaoe of abode of the person nominated, the mistake could be rectified. He conten- ded that the person nominated, and the proposer and seconder, had been deprived of their rights as citizens. Mr. SPENCE said that his father had been served in a similar way to Mr. Hankey. The CHAIRMAN said he was afraid they were not in order in discussing the proposition until it was secon- ded. It impugned the decision oi the chairman, and as chairman, he contended he was in order in disqualify- ing the nomination paper, because it did not give the name and address of the person nominated in the proper columns. As chairman he took the full responsibility of his action. If he had been approached at the meet- ing the paper might have been altered, but it was cer- tainly not the duty of the chairman to say that the correction should be made. Mr. SMITH: There were full instructions on the paper as to how it was to be filled up. The CHAIRMAN (continuing) said he would not accept the extract from the Local Government Act given by Mr. Jenkins unless he had had time to consider it. Nor would he accept what Mr. Jenkins had said as law. The portion of the Act which Mr. Jenkins had quoted, might not cover the case in point. If he had been asked at the meeting to alter the; paper, he would have seen what could be done. - Mr. Vilareing : Mr. Hankey got up and asked about it, but the Clerk said it was out of order. According to Mr. Jenkins, it was not out of order and might have been amended. The Chairman: I wasn't asked as Chairman to put it in order. No one regretted more than I did that this nomination paper was not in order. There was no seconder to the resolution and the matter dropped.

20TH CENTURY INSURANCE.

The status of the Eagle Insurance Company (remarks the "Insurance Record") has steadily improved during the last few years, the sound and cautious policy of the officials having been backed up by the influential Board of Directors with a result gratifying to all connected with the Company. The Eagle may be regarded,indeed, as well on the up grade, and should in the near future be able to give an even better account of its doings than was presented at the recent annual meeting held at the Head. Office, 79 Pall Mall, London. The net new business was 38 per cent. in advance of the figures for 1902, and 50 per cent. better than the average of the last ten years. No doubt the handsome bonus has helped in the influx of business, and everyone about to insure should read the Twentieth Century Prospectus, which includes all the best features of Life Insurance up to date.

Mr. Pierpoint M.P.

addressed the following letter on Tuesday last to our member and I append his reply. The nature of the Bill introduced on Wednesday by Hr. Akers- Douglas is a strange comment upon the last sentence of Mr. Pierpoint's letter. I hope his constituents will have something to say about his action before long.— Yours, &G.,

jOEIN FRANCIS MATTHEWS. [coPv.l "Baptist Church, Latchford, Warrington, April 19th, 1904. Dear sir,—According to present arrangements Mr. Balfour is to introduce his Licensing Bill in the House of Commons to-morrow. You are aware that his intention is to seriously curtail the power of the licens- -injustices, and in addition to provide a compensation fund for surrendered licences. In view, therefore, of the gravity of the issues involved, may I•express the hope that you will beable to meet the wishes of a large number of your constituents by being in your place and voting against the •measure ?—I ant, very truly yours jalflS FRANCIS MATTHEWS, minister, president of the Warrington und.District,Temperance Federation, Robert Pierpoint, Esq., M.P.

[COPY.] "House of Commons, April 20th, 1901. Dear sir,—l have daly received your letter. The Bill which has been introduced this afternoon is, I think, conceived on lines to which little objection can be taken.—Yours faithfully. ROBERT PIERPOINr. To the Rev. John Francis Matthews." _ _

_ [ln the division list wenotice the name of Mr. Robert Tierpoint as voting in favour of the Bill.—En."Ex."] Chinese Labour in the Transvaal. Sir.--Theletter of Mr.F.B.Grimshaw in your last week's Issue is of interest because of his personal knowledge of the conditions in the Transvaal. but ills arguments must be judged upon their merits, and I fail to see that his letter does anything to strengthen the plea for the introduction of Chinese Labour.

To my query as to why it was impossible to work the mines in South Africa with white labour, as in other paris of the world, if similar conditions as to wages and surroundings were created, Mr. Grimshaw replied "Because white men cannot live decently under £l6 a month, and the percentage ef gold obtained from the ore is so small and the depth of the working so great." Taking the last point first,l would remind Mr. Grim- shaw that there are other parts of the world where the depth of the workings isas great and which are success- fully worked by white labour. In Victoria for example the mines are worked to a deeper level than in the Rand, and these are worked by white men at trades -union rate of wages. This point recalls the words of Mr. Rudd, one of the directors of the Consolidated Goldfields Company. "If 200,000 native workers were to be replaced by 100,000 whites they would simply hold the Government of the country in the hollow of their hand, and, without any disparagement to the British labourer, I prefer to sea the more intellectual section of the community at the helm." Mr. Tarbutt another director of the same com- pany spoke in the same strain, concluding with the words "The combination of the labouring classes vill become so strong as to be aisle more or•less to dictate, not only on questions of wages, but also on political questions by the power of their votes Vim a Represen- tative Government is established." Can these representatives of the "more intellectual section of the commanity " as they call themselves, object if we take them at their wcrrd,and learn from them that erre of their objects in seeking to introduce low priced labour is that they may retain the dominating power over all the material affairs of that part of the Transvaal where gold is both king and god? The oeeond reason that Mr. Grimshaw gives why whites cannot be employed is that the percentage of gold is so small. I readily admit that there are some mines containing low grade ore which it would not be profitable to work with white labour. This also is true of many mines of different classes in this conntry. Would Mr. Grimshavv because of this,introduce Chinese labour into the mining industries of this country? But it is well to recall the fact that all the world over where industries appeared doomed by the rise in labour cost, frequently through the withdrawal, because of humane reasons of children's or female labour, that the mines of those interested being compelled to use bettermethods of utilizing, the forces of man or nature have devised contrivances which have made the doomed industry more prosperous than ever. Take coal mining in this country; it is not many years since children and women carried, or pushed in trucks, the coals along the workings, and when 'legislature stepped in, there was a great outcry that the industry would be ruined if this cheap form of labour was not continued. But the industry has survived the change, and mechanical haul- age is now doing the work at less cost than the low pviced human labour of earlier days. In the Transvaal we are told that this old method of bringing the rock from the working to the shaft is still largely used, simply because the mineowners have not troubled to employ a better system, having low priced human labour at their call.

I come now to 111 r. Grimshaw's third reszon why white labour cannot be emPloyed, which is "Because white men cannot live decently under £l6 a month." And why not? Simply because everything that is necessary for life is so dear. And what makes these dear? Is it not the high cost of procuring,trans- porting, and distributing these articles? If we trace the passage of goods from, say, England to Johannes- burg, we will find that monopolies levy their tribute and increase the cost of the goods at every point. A shipping ring controls the passage of merchandise to the cape; taxes, high railway charges add to the cost right along the journey, while the land monopoly in Africa, as everywhere else, levies its blackm-til on all that is done on it. _

I have dealt with what appears to the the most im- portant part of Mr. Geirashaw's letter. With the opinions of a section of the Nonconformist ministers of the Rand, lam not much concerned. Never yet has there been any great wrong which has not had some ministers ready to defend it. In the American slave trade, ministers were its strongest defendants, and claimed a special authority because they said their arguments were furnished from the Holy Scriptcres. We are ready iespectfully to accord to these men their right to their opinions, but we respect their arguments only for what they are worth; we decline to accord to them any special right, because of their cloth, to dictate to us on matters of right or wrong. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, April 21st, 1904.

Councillor Dodds and the Policy or Extravagance Sir,—lt was with feAings of astonishment,commingled *with some little amusement, that, on picking up my 'last Saturday's "Examiner," I found that the above loquacious gentleman, who promised so very much to the overburdened ratepayer last November, and which ended with such disastrous results to him and his party. still entertains some hopeof being rehabituated in the public confidence, by making another *impeachment on the old malady. In discussing the estimates for the current year, he tells us that he has found the cause for all our reckless extravagance in the fact that we have allowed Moses to be nursed by his own mother. Well, I have heard somewhere that this lady was a winning old dame, but I never expected for one moment that she would turn out to be the Father of the Council, or even Chairman of the Finance Committee, and that she .would scarcely have the audacity to audit the accounts of her own committee. And yet after all, there is nothing so very extra- ordinarily wonderful in this, whea we come to remem- ber that Mr. Dodds, who ought to know so much about figures, but has not the time, should allow this old lady to cook things her own way, and then also keep back the information, in what measure and to whom we are specificalir indebted for the filching of her public pie. ilt s true he makes a vague sort of statement 'that the policy of extravagance and waste is due to people inside and outside of the Council, trying to do something for their personal friends, brothers, and brothers-in-law,, relations, and friends generally," dad also that he had stated all this before publicly, but it had all been stopped now. Well, I claim to have a very fair memory,, especially for anything Mr. Dodds may have to say res-, pecting such an important matter as this, but I cannot call to mind Lay one occasion when he has givent utterance in public, to any statement which could be reconciled to the one I have just mentioned. There- fore, I unhesitatingly say that it is not only vague, but positively untrue. If he has the courage of his opinions let him make bold the statement in public, and not covet himself within the compafis of the four Council walls, in giving it utterance, which, he knows too well, affords him every protection, to make charges and abuse those outside. If he will do this, and make good the statement (that certain men have utilized their public position in and out of the Council, to get work and orders for their business to the tune of £20,000 out of the new police buildings) then I can assure him that he will not only gain the gratitude, but the confidence of these ratepayers, on whose behalf he claims so much. It is very little use telling the philosophical ratepayer that our expenditure is now almost appalling, but that he. not having the time, cannot suggest the remedy,and he certainly cannot plead inquiry, as that little item of £5OO very well shows. And so, this is how we stand to-day, nothing yet but vague charges, with a smack of the old type, which after all, cannot count for much; for if we must judge these men's magnanimity and advocacy of the public good, by the measure of their sympathy towards one of their own body, who took upon himself their legal responsibilities, then I say, no one can feel surprised that they stand la tho 4opeiess condition in which they

===== 0002601_19040423_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
find themselves placed to-day.—Thedems ycoa ittr tion in your nest issue, I beg to reolain,Yours fkithfully, THOMAS KNOWLES.

A Suggested "Esperanto " Society;

for Warrington.

Sir,—May I be permitted to avail myself of your columns, to respectfully suggest the formation of an "Esperanto "Society, for Warrington and District? Many of your readers may not have previously heard of "Esperanto:" so—with your permission—l will endeavour to give a brief summary of the subject : "Esperanto" is the name of the most perfect attempt, hitherto invented, to provide mankind with a second language. We are all aware that—by the aid of their secondary language, known as Yiddish—the Jewish com- munities of all countries can freely intercommunicate with each other, at sight. Very well, "Esperanto" is a thoroughly practical solution of the problem of con- structing a "composite' European language, which will satisfactorily serve the same purpose—to mankind in general—as Yiddish admittedly does for the Jewish community. Already, about one hundred thousand persons, in various countries, are familiar with "Esperanto." Societies have been established in many towns in England—including the neighbouring towns of 'ladders • field. Keighley, and Leeds. I venture to think that we, in Warrington, should try to keep ourselves up-to-date, and abreast of the times, in intellectual pursuits, quite as earnestly as we do in physical games. Livevpool establishished an "Esperanto' Society;this week (Wed- nesday). Dr. R. J. Lloyd occupied the chair, and gave an address on the merits of the new language.

U...• • •-• t.l One of the greatest scientific men of our day—Sir William Ramsey, K.C.8.--is an enthusiastic admirer of "Esperanto," as indeed, any man or woman will be, who gives half-an-hour's serious consideration to the elementary principles of the new language. Thera are no exceptions or irregularities in the grammar; the spelling is strictly phonetic; each letter has only one sound; and, singular to relate, the "Esperanto" alphabet and vocabulary are so scientifi- cally constructed, as to blend or fit in with the pro- nunciation and root-elements of the principal European languages. • "Esperanto "words are" built "upon a sslid found- ation of definite and invariable laws. The best points of many languages are artistically and intellectually welded into a perfect "composite "whole. The system is so simpleas to be within the easy reach of the boy or girl of eight, or of the hale man or woman of eighty. Commencing in early boyhood with Greek—it has all my life been a pet hobby of mine to study the elemen- tary structure of various languages, and speaking with this experience, I conthlently state that I have never met with any language that even approached "Esper- anto" in flexibility, simplicity, and consistency. "Esperanto" is suitable alike for commerce, literature, poetry. science, and general correspondence and con- versation. _ _ _

The author of "Esperanto," is a medical doctor, named Lazaro Ludoviko Zamenhof, who has devoted— from youthful student period—the best years of his life, to working out the problem of a universal language. So long ago as 1878, the language was more or less ready, but the doctor determined to further test his new language, and to work it out fully and practically. Whilst still in his early prime, the doctor has now the proud satisfaction of seeing "Esperanto" taken up, seriously. Enthusiastic so3ieties have been formed, in many countries of thebitrld; and "Esperanto" has even been adapted to the Braille instruction book for the blind. Like our own Tobias Smollett, M.D., Dr. Zamenhof, of Warsaw, will—as time rolls on—be recog- nized as one of the immortal literary adornments of the noble medical profession. "Esperanto "dictionaries and magazines have sprang into existen3e, in England and on the Continent, and it is being realised, by the wall-informed portion of the public, that " Esperanto " is as vastly superior to " Volapuk "—as an ideal universs.l or international language—as Pitman's Phonography is to the crude short-hand systems that preceded it. Stirely,no stronger recommendation need be adduced.

Sir William Ramsay tells us that attempts are nikav being made to get ths Association of Academies, or learned Societies, which will hold a meeting in London in June, to take up the question,with the ultimate object of inducing the various Governments to move in the matter, and to make the" Esperanto" language a com- pulsory subject in schools. Should that be successful, the next generation will find themselves able to com- municate freely with each other, front the North Cape to Gibraltar, from Galway to Odessa. Sir William adds that fairly rapid progress is being made; no fewer than 15a societies, with a membership of many thousands, have been formed, and they receive recruits daily. Inpassing. I would record a curious fact, and one which would no doubt be surprising to Dr. Zarnenhof himself—that is to say, the striking affinity of many of the root words of " Esperanto " with the root-words of the ancient Celtic language (what I happen to speak with tolerable flueney) of the wastera portion of these islands. I can only account for this singular coinci- dence of affinity, by the fact that the language of the ancient Britons is, itself, in strong affinity to one of the greatest of the world's foundation-languages— Hebrew. No wonder that many believe that the Ten lost tribes of Israel settled in these islands—a subject I have often heard discussed, but can express no definite opinion upon. The affinity of language is a fascinating chapter in this world's romantic story of Man and his migrations. • Some Of your readers PI ly be interested in the brief oatline I hive given, and ally wish for further • infor- mation or illustrative examples of the "build " of " Esperaeto." In my leisure evening hours, I shill be pleased to reolv to any ellairers who 01.31.093 StAraDBd addressed envelopes. One evening a week (say 7.30 to 9.30), for six months, would be quite sulcieat to mike marked progress in such a simplified language as " Esperanto; anti si 311 d be glad to help to form a free evening class, either at my house or at any more suitable place, with the view of establishing a Warring- ton" Esperanto "society. Just incidentally, while my pen is in my hand, I would say that I have One evening each wsek at liberty for a plucky few who would care to terra a metual im- provement class in elementary Jap inese.Sorne years ago, in tacheater, I had a colleague who had lived a long period in Japan—(Mr. Joseph Morris, author of the charming Japanese novel. "Kotaka, a Samurai's daughter ")—and I acquired—especially as my then chief, Kenneth Aaclver, Wog., had been eight years in China,—a taste for these extremely difficult languages of the Far East. For commercial purposes, we British are hopelessly behind Germans, Swiss, and Russians; in the acinisition of languages. Japanese will become the ruling language of the Far East, for trading require- 'resents, and therefore our various Chambers of Com- merce should enceurage students to take up a language which will—later on—spell trade prosperity in Eastern markets. Probably the British nations will leave both this much-needed Eastern trade, and the drudgery of learning Japanese, to the more plodding, practical, and enterprising Germans. Returning to" Esperanto," I would like to quote Sir William Ramsay,K.C.B., where he reminds us that" No attempt is being made to displace any modern language. The men and women of each country will still continue to speak their native language; but, at the same time, a knowledge of "Esperanto"—as. a universal second language—will enable them to converse with and to write to, those speaking a different tongue." Sir William's personal opinion is, that England—owing to our stiff national Conservatism—will be one of the last to join this movement, but he earnestly pleads for our careful consideration of so desirable an object. The New Auxiliary International language, is as intellect- ually valuable—as a thoroughly sound and legitimate process of mental training—as it is ingenious and practicable. Those who have studied " Esperanto " will, I am sure, agree with me in their estimate of—(as Lord Byron said of Sophia Lee's masterpiece " Kruitz- ner ")—the singular powers of mind and conception which it develops. Dr. Zaraenhof, the distinguished author of "Esperanto," philosophically remarks that "Language is the prime motor of civilization, and to it alone we owe the fact that we have raised ourselves above the level of other animals. Difference of speech is a cause of antipathy—nay even of hatred, between people, as being the first thing to strike us on meeting. Not being understood, we keep aloof, and the first notion that occurs to our mind is—not to find out whether the others are of our own political opinions, or whence their ancestors came from, thousands of years ago, but to dislike the strange sound of their language." Personally. I believe that if ever mankind give up war, it will be largely due to the influence of a universal language bringing about a universal brotherhood. We see the vast influence of langinge,in keeping the Anglo- Saxon community at pea-ce with itself, and just in the same way, we may be sure that "Esperanto" will surely tend in the direction of removing—or at any rate of materially subduing —racial animosities and misunder- standings.—Yours faithfully, H. P. HUGHES,

A joint meeting of the Whitecr)ss and St. Austin's Liberal Ward Committees was held on Wednesday evening at the Workingmen's MisQion, Thewlis street, for the purpose of electiog ward committees and representatives to the Association for the ensuing year Tea was served in the schoolroom, at which about 120 workers sat down. Mr. Councillor James Evans catered in his usually satisfactory rcianner, and the cammittees subsequently adjourned to separate school- rooms for the transaction of the business in hand.

The meeting of the Whitecross ward committee was presided over by Councillor James Evans. Several letters of apology for non-attendance were read, in chiding one from Councillor B rough who stated that he had received an injury to his leg in an accident,and the doctor advised him not to come out.

Councillor James Evans was re-elected Chairman, and the following were appointed vice-chairmen: Councillors W. Brough. and T. Barton, Mr. H. Roberts, J.P., Mr. William Wright, Mr. Nathan Cook and Mr. E. Osborne. The hon. secretary (11r. James T. Barton) and the assistant secretary (tr. Robert W. Cook) were re-elected. The committee and representatives were appointed and several new names sere added to the list.

The St. Austin's Ward Committee was presided over by Mr. Councillor Tinnion (chairman of the com- mittee). The officers .ald committees were re- appointed and various suggestions for improving the organisation were 'considered. A joint meeting of th 3 two committees was after- wards held and the g tthering was addressed by Mr. A. H. Crosfield. Mr. Cmprsillor Tinnion presided and he was accompanied on the platform by Mr. Crosfteld, Mr. Councillor Burton, Mr. Cona.lillor James Evans and Mr. C. Jackson Holmes.

The CFLAIRMAS referred to the impending gen&ral election, which he considered could not be much longer delayed. If Mr. Balfozir. succeeded in keeping the Government together for another twelvo months , that would be the fullest li Mit, and they would have to put `up with another twelve months of mess and muddle. It behoved the Liberals of Warrington to be ready fora contest at any moment, and he believed that in the Whitecross and St. Austin's wards some of the best fight- ing material in the town was to be found. If properly organised he believed they would be able to give a very satisfactory account of themselves at the next general election. (Hear, hear.) SPEECH BY KR. ARTHUR CROSFIELD.

Mr. ARTHUR CROSFIELD who had a very cordial reception, said: I understood that the meeting to-night was more for busin ss than for oratorical purposes and I have not come here with any cut and dried speech. I propose briefly to look over the main situation which we have to deal with. Let us take stock of the situa- tion, knowing that it cannot be more than twelve months before we have to fight a political battle which may be the turning point, and I hope will be the turn- ing point, in the political history of Warrington, not for the moment only, but I trust for a great many years to come. (Hear, hear.) I heard a little conversation the other day between a Conservative friend of mine and Sir John Brunner, and this friend of ours on the other side, like a good many Conservatives is getting a little uneasy at the state of affairs which this reactionary Government has landed us in. He made a rather half-hearted admission on the matter. The finances of the country, he thought, were not very sound, and he felt that we had done enough of what John Burns describes as plundering and blundering. (Laughter.) This Conservative friend did not exactly describe it in those words but that was about what it amounted to. Sir John Brunner turned to him and said: If .you want to have this country decently governed and properly administered you must have 20 years of sound Radical government. (Hear, hear.) At the Parr Hall a few weeks since, I said there were several great topics before us, any one of which was sufficient to inspire all of us to do our utmost on behalf of the Progressive party. (Hear, hear.) The causes to which I allude are not great academic abstractions; they are causes vital to the whole of this nation, and in a particular degree to the inhabitants of our large town and our industrial classes. (Hear, heir). The main issue that is going to predominate over everything else is the great issue of

===== 0002601_19040423_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE TRADE.

Happily there are a great many man in this country of such public spirit that they are going to fight this battle with us. Not alone those who have been with us in the past, but many of those who have been opposed to us. We are going to have a great rallying to the pro- gressive standard on the great issue of Free Trade. (Hear, hear.) I have had the advantage of discussing the subject with a great many people during the last few months from every point of view. I am sorry to say that amongst some manufacturers there is a great hankering after this ten per cent pro- tection on manufactured goods. If it had been put to the country that we needed to consider the question of subsidies to shipping companies, which I know damage the iron trade in England and affect the output of wire, that would have been a matter for legitimate investi- gation. Every impartial man would have been willing to give that question fair consideration As Mr. Asqiiith told 1113 in the course of a conversation on the subject, we would be willing to consider a matter of that kind, but that was not protection. (Hear, hear). Against protection of any shape or form, as Free Traders we absolutely drew the line. The question of subsidies to steamship lines, or the question of the exoeptionally advantageous terms which some of our Euglish railway companies give to foreign manufacturers are matters which we would have been willing to con- sider. Above all we were quite willing to consider as soon as they liked, the question of mining royalties which amount to as much as ss. or Gs. a ton on the manufactured article. Whenever one of my protec- tionist friends says he has a hankering after gas ten 'per,cent., I say,very well then, are you willing to face the logical conclusion and go in for the taxation of food Stuffs. The agricultural interest is the back-bone of the Tory party and not for one instant will the agricul- tural interest stand a ten per cent duty on manufactured articles without they have food stuffs protected. (Hear, hear.) That is a very simple issue indeed. Ask your friends whether they are willing to face that taxation of food, and whether they have considered what it means to the 4,000,000 men , women and children who are engaged in the vast cotton industry, 80 per cent, of the products of which is exported to foreign markets with a tolerably narrow margin of profit. If you are going to increase the cost nf living to the people engaged in that industry you are going to inflict great misery on the people of Lancs.- Aire. (Hear,hear.) lam bound to say that if you put that question fairly and spuarely before the people who want protection, they as a rule flinch from facing such a situation. We have a perfectly simple issue in this case, and Free Trade being the main question before us at the next election,we have a magnificent rallying cry, not only for our own party, but for many people who at previous elections have beenagainst us. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19040423_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LICENSING BILL. ••=1..,•.... What It Involves and What People

Think of it.

The following are the main features of the Licensing Bill which Mr. Akers-Douglas, Home Secretary, intro- duced on Wednesday night in the House of Commons :- 1. Licences are to have security of tenure, except where misconduct is proved. 2. Compensation is to be paid when licences are re- fused on public grounds, apart from miscondact. 3. The compensation fund is to be provided entirely by the trade. Contributions ranging from £1 to £lsoa year will be required fromall houses, according to their annual value. If the powers conferred are fully exer- cised an aggregate of over £1,000,000 a year will be available for compensation. Ante-1859 beerhouses are dealt with as well as others, but in their case the com- pensation payable will be on a higher scale. Generally, the measure of the compensation will be the difference between the value of the premises with a licence attached and their value without one, calculated as if the Act had not been passed. The owners of the pro- perty will contribute three-fourths ef the compensation fund. The money will be paid to persons interested in cancelled licences in proportion to be settled by quarter sessions' in default of agreement. 4. In districts other than county boroughs brewster sessions will have the same powers as now in cases of misconduct, but instead of refusing licenses by reason of redundancy they will merely have the power to tre- port to quarter sessions,which will decide these matters through committees. 5. Quarter sessions will also have the sole power to grant new licences. They are not to grant any,however except by way of barter for existing licences or on the payment of a lump sum or an annual payment. Moneys received in this way will go to the compensation funds which will be administered entirely by quarter sessions. 6. In county boroughs such as Liverpool and other large towns, the city or borough magistrates, sitting with the Recorder, will have the powers which in other places are conferred upon quarter sessions. 7. The bill will not apply to Scotland or Ireland. The expressions of opinion on the Bill have been as varied as numerous, and we append a few from local magistrates, together with the views of leading M.P.'s, and others.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. F. Monks, J.P.

Mr. F. Monks, J.P.. chairman of the Warrington Licensing Committee, says :—The Licensing Bill of the Government is a blunder. It assumes that the best men in the trade are being driven out of it by the action of the Licensing Magistrates. The bast men are getting out in consequence of the keen competition of brewers to buy them out at fancy prices. The tied house system will prove the curse of the trade, because it does not adrait of a retailer's profit sufficient to compensate for honest management, except where a large trade is assured. Brewers are being driven to accept a lower class of tenant who will work for a smaller profit, and this will entail closer police supervision. The tied house system lends itself to the worst featuves of management, to the keenest competition amongst brewers to get control of the retail trade, and destroys that sense of responsibility which is one of the greatest safeguards, for the proper and efficient management of such a business. How magistrates sit- ting in Quarter Sessions are better qualified to control renewals and transfers than the local magistrates who know all the conditions, I cannot see. As to the grant- ing of a new license in exchange for an old one, we shall very soon realize what a blander this will be. As to the question of compensation I don't see why either the trade, or the public should be called upon to make good the losses of brewers any more than any other industry, and more especially where this is the result of reckless and extravagant expenditure to secure what is nothing less than a monopoly. As far as I can judge, there is not a single redeeming feature in the Bill, and the sooner it is withdrawn the better both for the Government, and the trade which it is intended to benefit.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Thomas Birchall.

MR. THOS. BIRCHILL, J.P.,says:—lt seemsto me that the new Licensing Bill is nothing less than an insult to the magistracy throughout the Kingdom. The plea that licensing authorities have dealt harshly with the trade, is untenable, as reductions during the last 20 years have not averaged per cent. Finding, as magis- trates do, that something like two-thirds of the cases of crime and disorder with which they are dailyconfronted come from the use of intoxicants, it is very requisite that a business which can be productive of so much wrong-doing, should be under their immediate control, The enactment allowing appeals to Quarter Sessions was always an unreasonable one, acting as it does on the assumption that those living ata distance and having no direct knowledge of the special circumstances of a case, are better able to decide rightly than those on the spot who know all about it. Yet this absurdity is to be intensified, and Quarter Sessions is to be the only medium for obtaining new licences for all sorts of places in its jurisdiction. The Bill, as one of its worst features seeks to give a vested interest in licences, which they have never possessed before. For more than 400 years this has been tacitly acknowledged, and only of late with the encouragement of a needy Government bidding for the votes of a powerful trade, has it been seriously questioned. The question of compensation is rightly relegated to the trade itself but it will be a serious mistake if the reductions in licences are to be limited by the amount proposed to be set aside as compensation fund. I think there is another side to the question of compensation. I know houses in Warrington that 30 years ago their full value was £3OO, but having had the privilege of a licence granted to them, are now valued at fifteen times that amount. Where does compensation to the com- munity,which by granting the licence has given the possessor a fortune,come in?

===== 0002601_19040423_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. E. J. Guest, J. P.

Mr. E. J. Guest J.P. says :—I regard the Government Licensing Bill as an attempt to benefit the publican at the expense of the public, and when I say publican I mean the brewer, for most publichouses are now tied houses. Mr. Balfour's reply to Sir H. Campbell-Banner- man consisted of a number of sneers which to my mind were very uncalled for. "C. B." objected to the Bill as taking the power of veto out of the hands of local magistrates and transferring it to the Quarter Sessions. Mr. Balfour I suggested that "C B." could not be aware that the Quarter Sessions have already the pdwer to revise the decision of the local magistrates. "C. B. contended that the position of the local magistrates in relation to the Quarter Sessions, was similar to the position of the Judges in relation to the House of Lords. But Mr. Balfour could not see it and sneered. And because "C. B." was and I trust is in favour of local option,Mr. Balfour again sneered at him on the ground that local option would take the power of veto out of the hands of the local magistrates. As I under- stand "C. B's." position ,it is this--local lop- don is the ideal veto. But if local option cannot be obtained the second best arrangement is for the local magistrates to retain the power they.have. He believes in a local authority in preference to an authority that is not acquainted with local circumstances. lam fully at one with Sir H. Campbell-Bannerman and I think it shows the weakness of Mr. Balfour's position that he found it necessary to sneer. The" trade" is a legal trade, but it is a dangerous trade and is a source of great loss and suffering to the community at large. It needs to be limited as much as possible, and the public which suffers in so many ways ought to have the power to decide not only what new licenses should be granted, but also what existinc, licenses are unnecessary. At present, however, local option is not before us, though I hope it will 'be ere long. The present attempt to curtail the power of the local magistrates is absurd and wicked. They have not used their power in favour of the public; but have been most careful to consult the interests of the publican to a very large extent. The interests of the publican have stood in the way of many unnecessary licenses being refused. As far as lam able to judge, the magistrates did not need less power, but to be urged to use the power they possess for the welfare of the community. This Bill is the outcome of the interview Mr. Balfour had with the " Trade" deputation. It is a Bill which is unworthy, and which will, I trust, never become an Act of Parliament.

As to compensation this is a question that Parliament oughttnot to deal with at all. It is admitted on all hands even by the Home Secretary himself when introducing his Bill, that licenses are not a right but a privilege granted for one year and for one year only. If custom has given to some the idea that licenses have a vested interest,the sooner custom is altered the better. The rich brewers have had their compensation in the mono- poly they have enjoyed so long. They need no com- pensation. Rather should they compensate those who have been made poor and miserable as the result of their trade. If the Government see that they can draw from the trade any further taxation, this ought to go to the revenue of the country, and help to lessen the burdens which oppress the less wealthy part of the community. The matter of compensation should be left entirely in the hands of the Trade and ought not to interfere in any way with the discretion of the magistrates in dealing with licenses. Mr. Fulton Smith.

Mr. Fulton Smith. of Messrs.—peter Walker and Sons Limited, says :—From the condensed reports of the Bill it would be premature to pronounce any hard and fast opinion on the measure or on its ultimate effects. Suffice it to say that it is an heroic attempt to deal with a difficult problem burdened with old-time legislation which is inapplicable to present day needs. To gain statutory acknowledgment of the principle of compensa- tion is a step in advanceand in keeping with enlightened views of justice and equity. No doubt much discussion will centre around this particular phase in the Bill. It is considered by some that the Insurance Tariff is pitched too high, nor does it seem evident why so large a sum as a mi:lion must be set aside every 3 ear : while the duration of the impost is not indicated. If the Bill pave the way for farther legislation to clear from the statute book the cumbrous enactments of the past, in favour of clearly defined rules and regulations for the guidance of all concerned in the conduct of the trade, then it deserves most careful apd unprejudiced con- sideration instead of being threatened with strenuous opposition on every point.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Arthur Chamberlain.

A Birmingham correspondent has had a conversation with Mr. Arthur Chamberlain with reference to the Government Licensing Bill. It was a worse bill than he expected to see, he said, and added cannot believe that the magistrates of great cities like Liverpool, Manchester, and Birmingham will be satisfied to see their business taken out of their hands, and I cannot believe that the towns will consent to have their law and order and sobriety looked after by people who are not acquainted with the local needs. Asked as to the compensation part of the bill, he said :—lt is a fraud to talk of it as a bill for 00mp3n5ati0n. It is a bill for granting a vested interest to the publicans under the pretence of compensation. The fund that it is proposed to raise would only scratch the surface of reduction. I estimate that the licences of the country are worth £300,000,00) even now, when the tenure of the licence is uncertain; whereas if the bill were passed they would be worth £600,000,000. What is compensation to the extent of £1,000,000 out of £600,000,000? It means that the compensation fund would only provide for the reduction of one six-hundredth of the licences each year.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Lloyd George.

Mr. Lloyd George, speaking in London on Thursday night, said he would like his hearers to go carefully into the Licensing Bill. This was a much worse Bill than it appeared to be—like most other things of this Government. (Laughter.) It had Ilac! a very varied career. It had been bandied from brewer to Bishop for weeks. It had been calling now at the cloister' and the next moment at the pub. (Loud laughter.) And the result was that it had a mixed odour of oil and whisky. (Renewed laughter.)

Rev. R. J. Campbell. The Minister of the City Ta-aple, the Rev. R. J. Campbell, has said to an interviewer that he was glad that away was made towards a further reduction of licences. Compensation from the trade, he thought, should greatly facilitate this reduction, and was cer- tainly not unreasonable. Tne other innovations were of more doubtful value.

What Labour M.P.'s Say. The LAbour members of Parliament are all opposed ta the Bill.

MR. W. CROOKS, M. P. SAYS:" My first impression is that the compensation proposals won't work. Who is to be compensated? Will the brewer who owns the house or the man whe is running it gat the compensation? The fact that the 'trade itself is raising the money does not lessen the difficulty at all. I should not be surprised to hear the trade say that they are better off as they are now. "The interference with the discretion of the magistrates is serious. They know far more about the local needs than anyone at the quarter Sessions." MR. ARTHUR HENDERSON : "I am opposed to the Bill because it increases art evil which has always been associated with the administration of the liquor laws by transferring from the resident magistrates to the Quarter Sessions the power of refusing to renew licences. We generally find the Quarter Sessions prepared to set aside all opposition, however strong, and favour the trade. The principle of compensation, although the money is provided by the trade,is a recognition of a vested interest in the licence. This is contrary tobvery decision given since the pass- ing of the Licensing Act in 1823. The Bulls not a measure of temperance reform at all. Nor could it ever be anything of the kind in view of the motive which inspired it, as set out in the speech which the Prime Minister made in March last year. The only thought that appeared to exercise Mr. Balfour's mind was for the publican, and not the public." " MR. RICHARD BELL : "It is not the people who per- sonally conduct public houses who will get compensation under the Bill, but the rich brewers, who so often put a fictitious value on licensed premises. I protest against any interference with the magistrates discretion. Labeur men must oppose this favouring by the Government of a particu- lar trade." Why not Scotland and Ireland ?

A correspondent signing himself C.J.S. writes : "The proposals of the Government with regard to drink licences and compensatior are, in my humble opinion, simply oatrageous. They are even worse than the majority of temperance workers had ever imagined. Most of us expected a Bill somewhat on the lines of Sir W. Hart Dyke 's Suspensory Bill, and Mr. Butcher's Compensation Bill,but to have a Bill of the character of the one introduced by the Home Secretary on Wednesday was staggering in the extreme. But I do not think there is any cause for alarm on the appar- ently overwhelming in tjority which carried the first reading. True, it was a great blow to find 316 votes arrayed against 149, but if the temperance forces of the country are true to their colours, and stick to their guns, the Licensing Bill of 1901 will join a somewhat similar one introduced in 1890. It was a vary gold move on the part of the Government not to include Scotland and Ireland in the compensation scheme. Why did they exclude these two valuable countries? Was it that our splendid Government was afraid of the Nationalist and Scotch votes? Or do they think that the two birthplaces of whiskey are too insignificant to participate in these excellent (?) proposals. Eam anxiously awaiting a reply."

DEATH OF POLICE-SERGEANT HENRY CHENEY.

The Warrington County Borough Police Force has just lost one of its oldest and best known members in the person of Warrant Officer Sergeant Henry Cheney, who died at his residence, 1, Heaton-street, at 5.30 p.m. on Monday last. Sergeant Cheney suffered a heavy bereavement twelve months ago in the death of his eldest son, at the early age of 21. Since then the deceased officer's health had failed gradually, and last January he was compelled to go on sick leave. In February he was sent to the Police Convalescent Home at Harrogate, on a four weeks' admission , order, but he did not benefit by the change, and returned after a fortnight's stay in a very feeble state, continuing to sink until Monday last when he passed away in the presence of his. family, the cause of death being pulmonary tuberculosis. Sergeant Cheney was a native of Dalton, in the West Riding of Yorkshire, and previous to joining the Police he served for some years as warder under the Prison Commissioners, baying been attached in that capacity to the staffs of H.M. prisons at Kirkdale and Chester, two establishments which have since been closed. In 1882 he quitted the prison ser- vice and joined the Warrington Borough Police. In 1884 he was appointed first class constable, and two years later was promoted to. the rank Of patrol .sergeant. In 1900, on the retirement of ex-Sergeant Chantler, Mr. Cheney was . appointed warrant officer and court sergeant, a position which he held until his death. Besides other duties he was in charge of the Police Court, and was responsible for the safe conveyance of prisoners to H.M. Prisons at Liverpool and Manchester. Like most police officers of experi- ence, Sergeant Cheney was a man of practical humanity, and of a tactful kindly nature. In this respect it is no exaggeration to say that amongst those who will hear of his death with feelings of genuine regret are many men and women who have been, un- fortunate enough to pass through the dock of the Warrington Polio? Court, or have become acquainted with the interior of H. M. Prisons. Of a genial disposition, and possessed of a rare fund of humour, the deceased officer was popular among his comrades, and he always took a deep interest in any- thing tending to their welfare. He was a member of the Police Library Committee,and had been connected with the Athletic Club of the Force from its inauguration,hav- ing served for some years as its Assistant Secretary. He was also an enthusiastic fisherman, and a member of the Warrington Anglers' Associatioa. In the making and repairing of fishing rods, and in many other forms of handicraft, Sergeant Cheney showed great skill and ability. Indeed, the dexterity with which he handled tools of all kinds, and his tasteful workmanship, served to indicate that had he chosen any of the mechanical trades instead of the public service as an occupation, he would have become a skilled artisan of the first rank. The sergeant was only 50 years of age, and would in a few years have been entitled to a life pension. His wife predeceased him some eight years ago, but he leaves a family of two sons and three daughters with whom much sympathy is felt.

===== 0002601_19040423_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUNERAL.

Sergeant Cheney's remains were interred in Warring- ton Cemetery on Friday afternoon, the funeral party leaving the deceased's residence about 2.30. The cortege was headed by an escort of police under Chief Inspector Phillips, the customary honours being rendered at the house and at the graveside. The deceased's helmet and dress tunic were placed on the coffin, which was borne to and from the hearse by Inspector Rowbotham, Ser- geants Smith and Williamson, Acting-Sergeant Condliffe, and Constables Hardy and Corbett. The burial service wa,s-canducted by the Rev. D. R. James, of St. Peter's. The mourners were: Masters Harry and Edward Cheney (sons), Mrs. Brailev and Misses C. and M. Cheney (daughters), Mr. JOhn Cheney, Norrnanby, and Mr. Lloyd Cheney, Eston (brothers),. Mr. Harry Cheney Warrington (nephew). Mr. J. Bratley (son-in-law), &c. Despite the unfavourable state of the weather a large ithisers .gathered at the number of respectful sVrnP graveside. At the close of the service Mr. Councillor Carton, an intimate friend of the deceased officer, and a constant visitor to .him in his last illness, addressed a few words to those assembled: On the grave was placed a large number of beautiful wreaths from rela- tives and friends, prominent among them being a very fine one of lilies of the valley, wnite tulips and maiden- hair fern,from the members of the Warrington Borough Police Force, of all ranks,

Success of a Warrington Violinist. The following is an extract from the " Manchester Daily Courier."—" At Mr. Cross's Concert held in the Association Hall, Manchester on Saturday last, Master Tom Barton's interpretation of Dancla's Romance and Bolero was splendid. In the second movement he showed great mastery of the instrument, preserving a fine quality of tone even in the most intricate and difficult passages, and was vociferously applauded, being recalled three times."

LOCAL WEDDINGS. Ridgway—Roberts. The marriage was solemnized at St. James' Church,. Birkdale, on Wednesday, of Ernest Edward Ridgway, youngest son of Colonel Ridgway, of Grappenhall Lodge, and Pauline May Roberts, only daughter of the late Mr. Arthur Roberts, and Mrs. Roberts of the Heights, Lancaster-road, Birkdale. More than usual interest was taken in the happy event, and in the Sankey Bridges district, where the firm of Messrs. Clare and Ridgway has been established for a long number of years, there was a profuse display of bunting. A large congregation witnessed the ceremony. The church was decorated for the occasion with a choice collection of " palms and plants. The Rev. W. Brace.amp, vicar of St. Paul's, Warrington, was the officiating clergyman.. The bride was given away by her brother (Mr. Roberts) She was prettily attired in a dress of crepe-de-chine, with veil and wreath. Two bridesmaids were in attend- - ance, namely Miss Ridgway (sister of the bridegroom) and Miss Wilkinson, of Birkdale. They wore pink dresses with black hats trimmed with pink flowers. The duties of best man were satisfacorily discharged by Dr. Batchen, of Penketh. A reception was afterwards held at the house of the bride's mother, and later in the afternoon, the newly-married couple departed for• London en route for the South coast for the honeymoon.. The presents formed a very choice collection and included a silver revolving soup tureen from the office • staff of Messrs. Clare and Ridgway and a silver salt and L pepper service from the workpeople.

Haslam—Pigott. On Tuesday afternoon, at St. George's Church,. Hanover-square, London, W., a large aud fashionable assembly gathered to witness the marriage of Mr. Cyril Haslam, of Frodsham, and Miss Margery Pigott, younger daughter of the late Rev. Randolph Pigott, J.P. for Bucks, and Rector of Grendon Underwood, and Grendon Hall, Aylesbury, Bucks, and of Mrs. Pigott, of Brenley Kent, and Grendon, Bucks. The service was fully choral and the church was handsomely decorated. The ceremony was conducted by the Rev. Edward Howley Birley, M.A., vicar of Ellastone, Ashbourne, Derbyshire, assisted by the Rev. Edmund Vincent Pigott, M.A., vicar. of Trentham, Stoke-on-Trent, uncle (of the bride. The bride was accompanied by her brother, Captain Vincent PigottAlst Warwickshire Regiment, who, during the singing of the nuptial hymn conducted her to the chancel entrance, and there gave her away. She wore a dress of joie d' Alva,ielegantly trimmed with old family lace, and a full Court train of satin, ornamented with, old point lace and orange blossom. The old family Honiton lace veil covered a tiara of orange blossom. Her only ornament was a diamond, ruby and pearl pendant, the gift of her mother. She carried a beautiful bouquet composed chiefly of orchids, lilies of the valley, and white heather. The bridegroom, who had arrived some time previous to the bride, was supporte dby Captain G.. R. Crosfield, of Warrington, as best man. There were four bridesmaids, Miss C. C. Pigott, Miss M. Pigot (sisters of the bride), Miss A. C. Haslam, and Miss J. Haslam (sisters of the bridegroom). They wore beautiful dresses of white silk voile, arranged with lace flounces and pale green waist belts, and large white chiffon hats, ornamented with streamers - and yellow roses. The bridegroom's presents to them were gold curb bangles, set with rubies and pearls, and each carried a nosegay of yellow roses and lilies of the- valley, with knots of primroses attached to trails of green ribbons. Master Brian Burrowes (nephew of the bride) acted as page. Early in the afternoon, Mr. and Mrs. Cyril Haslam left for a honeymoon tour in North, Devon.

HEAD TEACHERS' SALARIES. The following resolution has been passed by the School Management Committee, that in applying the scale of salaries, each five years of service. up to a maximum of twenty years as a head teacher in the school in the Borough, with an average attendance of less than 500 children, be counted as one year of service under the committee, the salary in no case to be increased above the in ,ximum of the scale." COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.—The EMPLOYMENT OF CHILDREN ACT, 1903. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the Town Council of the Borough of Warrington, being the Weal Ant horny for the purposes of the abovd Act, have made the following BYBLAWS with 'respect to OTR NET TRADING ;- 1. No boy under the age of 12 shall be employed or engage in street trading. 2. Nolgirl under the age of 14 shall be employed in or Carry on street trading 3. No girl under the age of 16 shall be employed in or carry on street trading in or at the entrances t-) any railway station nor in the following streets or public places ; Scotland-road, Bylands-stre-t, Palmyra-square, Queen- street. Battersby-lane. and Marsh House-lane, and in or near the Fair Ground in Queen-street. 4. No person under the ago of 16 shall be employed in or carry an street trading after eight in the evening, between the Ist of October and 31st March, or after nine in the even- ing, between the lit of April and 30th September. S. No person under the age of 16 engaged in street trading shall enter any premises licensed for public enter- tainment, or for the sale of intoxicating liquor for con- sumption on the premises, for the purpose of trading or delivering goods. 6. No boy under 14 shall be employed or engage in street traciing on Sunday. 7. No girl under 16 shall be employed or engage in street trading on Sunday. 8. No child who is not exempt from school attendance shall be employed in or carry on street trading during school hours. REGULA.TIONS AS TO L MENDES. 9. No person under 16 shall be erno loyed or engage in street trading unless furnished with a licence from the Council. 10. A Menet, to engage in street trading sl.all not be refused by the Council to any boy between the ages of 12 ;and 16, or to any girl between the ages of 14 and 16, apply- ng for it, except on the ground that the applicant is by reason of physical or mental deficiency unfit to trade in the streets, or that he has not his parent's or guardian's consent to his lofting so employed 11, Licences shell be granted for a period of one year only, and shall expire on the 31st December. 12. Every licensed person wtile trading in the street shall wear, in the appointed way, the badge prescribed by the Council. 13. No charge shall be made by the Council for any licence I or badge. 14. The Cornell may suspend or revoke any licence if the holder is convicted of any offence ; or commits any breach of these Byelaws ; or in any way defaces, lends, or parts With his badge. Objections to the above Byelawa may be made by any person affected within 30 days after the date of this publica- tion, and if made must be sent in writing to the secretary of State and addressed to him at the Home Office, London. Dated this 23r.1 day of April. 1904 S. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk.

Tom Bridge, respectively, with Mr. J. Wilkins and Mr. Simister as assistants. Given fine weather, the competition promises to be an exceptionally keen one. The green is in excellent condition, and Mr. F. W. Scarisbrick, the energetic hon. secretary, has made full arrangements to ensure that the contests shall pass off without the slightest hitch. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. The first match under the auspices of this newly-formed league was played on Saturday, , when Fearnhead defeated the Bull's Head team after an interesting encounter. A capital list of fixtures has been arranged, and a successful season . is anticipated. There are eight clubs in the League, namely, Fearnhead, Bull's Head, Horse and , IJockey, Liberal Club, Chapel House, Brooklands,Ceme- , I tery, and Penketh Park. Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, the presi- dent, has kindly consented to give a silver cup and medals for the winners,and the runners-up will doubtless receive medals from the League. The following matches will be played to-day. Horse and Jockey v. Liberal Club. Chapel House v. Brooklands. Cemetery v. Penketh Park. trThe League secretary is Mr. E. Taylor, Manchester- road ; the treasurer, Mr. J. Earle, Brooklands Hotel ; and the committee is composed of two members of each . club, with Mr. G. F. Freeman as chairman. Mr. Clare has granted the association a most useful i concession—that of reserving the green on Fridad even- ; ings specially for the teams who will ;be playing the i following day. This will give the players an opportun- ity to practice in preparation for the encounters of the ' following day. . Main at the Conservative Club. INSTANT RELIEF, RAPID CURE 0, GOUT AND RHEUMATISM. BY BADE'S PILLS. A RECORD CURE ! 32, Archer-Street, ;Bayswater, London, W. Dear Sir,—Sunday night my big toe pained me, on Monday it was much worse, and in the evening I could scarcely walk. At 6 p.m. I sent for a bottle of your Gout Pills, and took two at once. At 9.30 1 was much relieved, and could eat my supper. I took another before going to bed, and although so hot I got a fair night's rest.
